[trialAccount t18300916-259]
1723. MICHAEL LANE and MARY LANE were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of August, 1 coat, value 18s.; 1 pocket-book, value 6d.; 1 Prayer-book, value 4d., and 1 half-sovereign, the property of Timothy Lehane.
TIMOTHY LEHANE. I had this property in a basket, in my bed-room, at the prisoner's; I put the half-sovereign into the pocket-book at seven o'clock on Saturday, the 7th of August - I went out and came home in an hour or two, and went to get a razor out of the basket; I missed the coat and other articles - I asked for them, but got no answer; I made a great bustle about it, and as I was going out, about ten o'clock, they said they had pawned the coat; I then asked for the other things, and they said I should not have a halfpenny worth of them till I paid them a little balance which I owed them of 11s. 6d.; they did not say what they had done with the things in the pocket.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did not you owe them 1l. 1s. 6d.? A. No; there is no such lodger as Hannah Calley - I do not know whether I said at first that there was a sovereign; I do not know what I said at first: I never intended to say a sovereign - I went to Cambridge, and had to borrow the money to go; I did not go to a Magistrate, because I was dubious of the law - I went to Hatton-garden, and they told me to give charge of them; the Magistrate let them out on bail - I never told this woman she was welcome to pawn the coat to get some money out of the rent I owed; I meant to pay her at the rate of 1s. 6d. a week when I was in work; she gave me my lodging and my potatoes for dinner when I was out of work - I wrote this agreement, but she has not signed it; I wentand asked for my property - the prisoner and his wife came out, and she said she would throw the boiling water in my face; I suppose there were two hundred people in the street - I came home from Cambridge on the Sunday, and went about this on the Monday; he was taken in two or three days.
COURT. Q. Did they tell you where they had pawned it? A. No - I inquired at several pawnbrokers, but the officer found it; I had paid 5s. that same evening - it was 3s. 6d. for the week, and 1s. 6d. off my debt.
JOSEPH JOHN GOODGE. I have a coat, pawned by the female prisoner, on the 9th of August.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you known her before? A. Yes, and knew her residence; she gave her own name.
TIMOTHY LEHANE. This is my coat - the pockets are empty now.
MILES STANLEY. I took the prisoners - they gave me this Prayer-book, duplicate and letter; they said there was no such thing as a pocket-book or a half-sovereign.
Cross-examined. Q. Then they told you at once about it? A. Yes, and said the man owed them money, and they had pawned it - they were held to bail.
COURT. Q. When did the prosecutor complain to you? A. In September, I think - I went to the prisoners, and they wanted to explain the matter.
Witnesses for the Defence.
ANN KELLY. On the 8th of August I saw the prosecutor going down the court - he said, "Mrs. Lane, you are very well paid - there was a sovereign in the pocket;" when he came from the country I heard him say, "I am going to take them up for the half-sovereign;" I said,"You ought to stick to the same story;" the Magistrate let the woman out, and he wanted to bring a man to say he had the half-sovereign, but they would not let him - he said, "I have said it was a sovereign, but now I can tell what I like."
MARY EARLY. I live in the same court as the prisoners do. On the Sunday, when he left their house, I heard him call them different improper names - he said, "You have got the coat and a sovereign;" I went down to Worship-street, and said, "Did not you say it was a sovereign?" he said, "Yes - if there is occasion I can say what I like."
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-260]
1724. ADAM MARSHALL and JOHN MARSHALL were indicted for stealing, on the 20th of July, 6 loaves of bread, value 2s., the goods of William Fittock.
MARY FITTOCK. My husband's name is William - he is master of a Hambro' trader. On the 20th of July I caught John Marshall, who is a soldier, going out of my house in Grafton-street, Soho, at six o'clock in the morning, with something in a bag - I did not see what was in it, but I gave him in charge of the Policeman; the other prisoner is my journeyman - I keep a baker's-shop: I suspected he had given him the things in the bag.
JOSEPH TILLEY. I am a tailor, and live in Grafton-street, opposite the prosecutrix's; I saw John Marshall coming out of her house five minutes after six o'clock, with a bundle across his shoulder - she immediately followed him, tapped him on the shoulder, and said, "Now I have caught you in your villiany," and she gave him in charge.
JOHN JULIAN. I am a Police-officer. I found six 2lbs. loaves in the bag; I showed them to the prosecutrix at the watch-house, and she said they were hers - I went to her house, and she gave me charge of Adam Marshall; he went on his knees, and begged forgiveness - he said he had committed an error, and robbed a good mistress.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Did you state this before the Magistrate? A. Yes; Adam handed me 2s.; I did not go into the shop; I went into the bake-house - the prosecutrix said, "John Marshall has taken out six loaves, and this man gave them to him;" I said he had better put on his things and come with me - he said,"I am very sorry, I have taken 2s. of the other man for them; here is the 2s. which the soldier has given me - I know I have committed myself;" he did not say he took the 2s. for the bread.
MRS. FITTOCK. I saw some loaves at the watch-house, but I could not swear they were mine; the price of them was 2s. 0 1/2d., and Adam Marshall laid that money down to pay for them - the Policeman took up the 2s., and said, "Never mind the 1/2d.;" he said he was very sorry he had committed himself that morning; he said he had served the soldier with six 2lbs. loaves, and he had paid him for them - he begged me to forgive him.
Cross-examined. Q. Then the value of them was 2s. 1/2d.? A. Yes; it was taken from the dough-trough, and the soldier was a customer.
COURT. Q. Did the soldier tell you what he had in the bag? A. Yes; he said bread, but he had paid for it.
Adam Marshall's Defence. The soldier was taken in charge before I knew any thing about it; if the woman had come down I should have given her the money - he used to come and have the bread out of the bake-house; I said, "Here is the money for the bread."
John Marshall 's Defence. I am allowed to sell bread, coffee, and other things; I went to the prosecutrix's for bread, and said I would pay for it once a week, but the men did not pay me as I wished, and I went to her, and said I could not pay the bill, but I paid part of it to Adam Marshall, and the prosecutrix said she had received it.
MARY FITTOCK. He always was a customer, and paid very honestly; I had told Adam Marshall to let him have bread - I did not wish to go before the Magistrate, but I was forced to go. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-261]
1725. WILLIAM HUTCHINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of August, 1 gown, value 2l., and 2 sheets, value 5s., the goods of Elizabeth Herring.
ELIZABETH HERRING. I am married - my husband is alive; his name is John.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-262]
1726. TIMOTHY REEDY was indicted for stealing, 74lbs. of lead, value 12s., the goods of Edward Jones.
JAMES PERRY. Mr. Edward Jones is contractor for iron pipes to the New River company. I missed a pig of lead from the works on the day in question; I had seen it safe between ten and half-past ten o'clock, at the end of our trench - I have seen it since at Marylebone-office - it belongs either to the New River company or to Mr. Jones; it has N. R. C. on it - they are cast in a mould; it was lead of this description - I have known the prisoner five or six years; I never saw the lead in his possession, or spoke to him about it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How do you know Mr. Jones is contractor to the New River Company? A. I am his brother-in-law; he is considered on his death-bed - the work is now left in my care - I am answerable for every thing; this was missed on the 12th of August - Mr. Jones was then at the New River-head; the prisoner was a deputy turncock - Mr. Jones did not tell me any thing about this piece of lead - I had sent it there that morning; I know he is the contractor.
COURT. Q. Are you foreman there? A. Yes; he buys the lead, and I pay for it.
WILLIAM HENRY. I am a Policeman. I was on duty in Church-way, St. Pancras, at a quarter past five o'clock in the evening, and saw the prisoner coming along with a piece of lead on his shoulder - I followed him perhaps three hundred yards; I then spoke to him - he said he had found the lead; I said it was false, as it had been evidently cut from some other piece; after some few words, he acknowledged he had got a piece more at home, and if I would go with him he would give it me; I went to take him home, but returned, and took him to our station-house, as he had once behaved rather rough to one of our officers - our superintendent directed me to go to his lodging, where I found his wife and children - I got the key of a little girl who was nursing one of his children: I there found this other piece of lead, and these tools by it, which I presume he cut the lead with.
Cross-examined. Q. Are there any lodgers in the house? A. Yes; I have seen him go into the house, and seen his head out at that window; I was behind him as he came out of his own court with the property - any one might have seen it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was going by and found this lead; I knew the party it belonged to, and thought it might fall out of one of the carts - I was going to take it there when the Policeman met me; I worked for the company upwards of six years, and I thought I would restore it to them.
WILLIAM HENRY. He was going from his own house, but not the nearest way to the New-river reservoir.
JURY to JAMES PERRY. Q. Where was the trench? A. At Battle-bridge, in the public road - they had not been using lead from the time I saw it till I went to dinner; they did not begin to use lead till two o'clock - a person named Duggin was left in care of the things, and he gave me the information - he might have had his back towards the lead while he was walking about to take care of the tools - I swear positively this is the lead that was missing, by the marks, and there was no other lead there that day; I sent three pigs that morning, and one was taken while we were at dinner.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there not lead missing before? A. Never, to my knowledge; there was a great deal of lead there which belonged to the company.
WILLIAM HENRY re-examined. Q. Have you stated all that passed between you and him? A. Yes; I am certain he did not say any thing else at all about it.
Q. I have your statement before the Magistrate; did he not say where he was at work? A. No; he told a brother officer that he had it from the gas-works at Battlebridge; he did not say he was at work there - he said it had tumbled out of a cart.
JURY. Q. What time did you go on duty? A. At eight o'clock in the morning; I did not see him go into his house.
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-263]
1727. SAMUEL SHIRLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of August, 1 sovereign, and 10 shillings, the monies of Robert Honiball.
ROBERT HONIBALL. I keep the Reformation public-house, in Long-acre . On the 21st of August, between four and five o'clock in the morning, I was in bed, heard some one go down stairs very softly, and heard the bar-door open; I got out of bed, and went down after him - I saw the prisoner, who was billetted at the house, at the till, and heard money jink; I was half way down the stairs; he was in the act of shaking a box; when he saw me he came to meet me; I said I had caught him - he begged I would forgive him - I said I would not; I called my son and my servants, but none of them heard; I staid a few minutes, but I was very unwell, and I allowed him to go back to his own bed-room; I called my servants and son again; I went to my own room and dressed - I then went for the Policeman, and gave him in charge - he was searched in about half an hour after I first saw him, but nothing was found on him but a sixpence, and a few halfpence - we never discovered any money on him - he said if I would allow him an hour and a half, he would bring the money that was lost - I missed a sovereign and 10s. out of a box which was in the till.
JOHN HONIBALL. I am the prosecutor's son - I was in the bar when my sister left at night, but I did not count the money.
JANE HONIBALL. I am the prosecutor's daughter. I saw the money-box in the till - I saw two sovereigns and about 10s. in it, between twelve and one o'clock - all the family were in bed but myself and brother - there was one sovereign, and about 4s. 6d. left in the till next morning.
WILLIAM HARRIS. I was sent for to take the prisoner - he said, if we would allow him an hour or an hour and a half, he would go to his brother-in-law's, and make the money good - he asked the prosecutor what he missed - he said, about 30s. he thought - I took him to the station-house, and searched him very strictly, but found nothing on him - I searched the room, but the money has not been found.
Prisoner's Defence. I had to get up early that morning, being one of the guard of honour - I went down, and saw the bar-door open - I was astonished; I looked in, and saw no one; I ventured in, thinking some person had broken in - I had not been there a minute before the prosecutor came and saw me - I told him I had seen the door open - I had gone down to wash.
JURY to JANE HONIBALL. Q. Did you lock the bar? A. Yes; and took the key up with me.
ROBERT HONIBALL. There is a partition on the top of the door, which can be easily opened, but the bottom part was not opened - I afterwards found a knife would open it - he had got over the half door; the till was not locked - I think it was necessary for him to go down to wash, but not to the bar; he seemed to go down softly - I think he had no shoes on.
GUILTY. Aged 34. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-264]
1728. JOSEPH STALLARD was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of August, 6 live tame geese, price 24s., the property of William Nicholls.
GEORGE NICHOLLS. I manage the business for my uncle, Mr. William Nicholls. I lost six geese on the 22nd of August; I had seen them on the day before - I have seen two geese since, but I cannot swear to them - they were white ones, and ours were all white - they correspond in size - I went to the watch-house, and found the prisoner there, with two geese that morning; he had been employed by my uncle - I have seen him in the stables, the premises are surrounded by a wall six feet high, with spikes at the top.
DENNIS BEALY. I am in the prosecutor's employ. On the 21st of August, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, the geese were all safe; there is no fastening to either of the doors of the kitchen where they were; I went out about five or half-past five o'clock in the morning; I found the pond had been dragged for fish, and I saw blood on four separate places at the end of the pond; I have seen the two white geese - one of them has a particular claw, which I had marked, as being one of the two small ones which I had kept back - I had twenty-five of them, and sent them up as they were wanted - they were dead when I saw them.
Cross-examined. Q. What is the mark? A. A hole in the claw - the Magistrate asked how I knew them; I said one had a topping on it; I said so at the watch-house, that I could swear to one of them; I do not know that I was asked about the feet - I went to prove the property; I do not suppose the Magistrate asked me how I knew them - one of the gentlemen asked me some questions - I do not know that I was asked to swear to the geese - I will not swear to the colour.
COURT. Q. What do you mean by saying you would not swear to the colour? A. They were mostly all white.
WILLIAM MASSAM. I saw the prisoner near Paddington, at five o'clock on Sunday morning, with a basket; I asked what he had - he said a hock of bacon and a bit of bread; I collared him - he said he would go with me, and I let him go; he ran into the canal up to his waist, ran down two hundred yards, and then trampled the basket under his feet in the canal; my brother officer took him, and I got a boat - a person drew the basket out with a stick; I took it to the watch-house, and found in it one small goose and one large one - one with the head off and one on; they were quite warm, and appeared lately killed- I afterwards saw the witness; I do not recollect that he gave me any description of the geese before he saw them - I produce the legs of the geese.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he describe them after he saw them? A. I did not hear him say any thing about them - I asked the prisoner where he brought the basket from; he said from Stone-bridge.
Prisoner. I was at large for six hours. Witness., My brother officer took him to the watch-house, and the inspector sent him back with the officer to find the property - he went into the water to find it, but could not; I went into the boat and got it, and the prisoner walked away - some of the men were to give him a glass of gin; he was taken the same morning.
Cross-examined. Q. Did your brother officer make the slightest attempt to prevent his going? A. No.
JURY. Q. Are you certain it was the same basket? A. Yes - I never left the spot till I found it.
GUILTY. Aged 31. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18300916-265]
1729. WILLIAM THOROGOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of August, 1 tub, value 2s., the goods of James Beal.
JAMES BEAL. On the 30th of August, about ten o'clock in the morning, I missed a tub from my shop; I had seen it a quarter of an hour before - a person came and told me of it; I went up the street, as I was directed, and found the prisoner fifty yards off with it.
RICHARD MILLER. I took charge of the prisoner; the prosecutor took the tub back - the prisoner said he had no tub; at that time the man had it again.
Prisoner's Defence. It was out at the door, and distress drove me to do it.
GUILTY. Aged 35. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-266]
1730. WILLIAM WHEELER and JOSEPH WARD were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of September, 1 pair of trousers, value 4s., the goods of William Mote and John Appleford.
THOMAS HURLEY. I am a law-writer. I was walking by the shop of Mote and Appleford, and saw Ward walking away with these trousers - the two prisoners were together; Ward first put them under his coat, and then handed them to Wheeler - they were both near the door when Ward took them; they were hanging rather inside the door, and fastened by a string - I did not see them cut down; I told Mr. Pyatt of it, and the prisoners were stopped in Exmouth-street - Wheeler threw the trousers and a pair of scissars away from him; Ward was taken in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour - there was no cry of Stop thief! they ran when the officer was in pursuit.
Wheeler. I was coming from Covent-garden, where I had been to buy some walnuts - he cannot say he saw me at the door. Witness. Yes; I saw him go, and ask the price of a handkerchief - I swear Ward gave you the trousers as you turned the corner.
Ward. He never saw me near the place. Witness. Yes - I am sure he is the person I saw walk away from the shop with the trousers.
MICHAEL RADFORD. I am an officer. On Wednesday, the 8th of September, a person told me he believed Wheeler had taken a pair of trousers; I crossed, and got within a few yards of him - he had them under his arm; he ran, and threw them down with a pair of scissars - I took him; he said he had picked them up - I asked why he ran; he said he was startled at the time - I took him towards the station, and my brother officer was called to take Ward.
WILLIAM BRYMER PYATT. I am in the employ of William Mote and John Appleford; the witness told me a pair of trousers had been stolen - there is a mark on them; they were on the door-post.(Property produced and sworn to.)
THOMAS MOOR. I am a constable. I took Ward; I saw Wheeler throw down the trousers - Ward was walking down Coppice-row; the witness said he was the man he saw at the door.
Ward. I was going to Smithfield - I was never in his company at all.
THOMAS HURLEY. I am quite positive he was the person.
Wheeler. I never ran; I stopped when the officer touched me.
WHEELER - GUILTY. Aged 20.
WARD - GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-267]
1731. MARY WATERS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of July, 1 counterpane, value 2s., and 2 sheets, value 2s., the goods of James Clark.
LYDIA CLARK. I am the wife of James Clark. The prisoner lodged at our house from October till the beginning of July, when she was taken for robbing us; she had not left the house, but she had not slept at home the night before.
DANIEL BROWN. I am an officer. I took the prisoner on the prosecutor's complaint; she denied having taken the property - I found the quilt in Laystall-street, not at a pawnbroker's.
GEORGE UNDERWOOD. I am a pawnbroker. The prisoner pawned a sheet on the 17th of May, and another on the 28th of June.
The prisoner delivered in a written Defence, stating that poverty had caused her to make use of the property, which the prosecutrix had allowed her to do on a former occasion.
LYDIA CLARK. I never gave her leave to pawn anything at any time.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Fined 1s. and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18300916-268]
1732. JOHN AGGUS was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 6th of September, of a certain evil disposed person, 12 boxes, value 12s., and 1.800lbs. of tinplates, value 30l., the goods of John Worrall Grazebrook, and George Munro, well knowing them to have been stolen, against the Statute, &c.
MR. DOWLING conducted the prosecution.
JAMES BROWN. I am an officer. I saw the prisoner; he said he wished me to go to Spicer-street, where he had some boxes of tin, as he had seen in the paper that some had been stolen, and he thought they might be them - he said he had received them from a man in Bethnal-green, who drew them to the Flower-pot; the man left him there, and he drew them to his own house.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Then he came of his own accord, told you he had the property, and the worthy Magistrate committed him? A. Yes, I have been searching for the other man, but cannot find him.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18300916-269]
1733. JOHN SAMUEL BLAIN, was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 6th of September, of a certain evil-disposed person, 9 boxes, value 9s., and 600lbs. of tin plates, value 20l., the goods of John Worrall Grazebrook, and George Munro, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. RYLAND conducted the prosecution.
JOHN WORRALL GRAZEBROOK. I am in partnership with George Munro. In the beginning of this month, we lost a number of tin plates, and on the 7th of September the prisoner called, and said he had a quantity of tin plates, and asked if we would exchange some of them - we said if they were our mark and make, we should have no objection; Mr. Munro asked him if he had any objection to tell who he got them of - he said he had not, but he should not like his name to be mentioned, and he would point out the house of the person he had them from - Mr. Munro said we had lost a great many, and he was suffered to depart; we saw him in custody two days afterwards - we saw the boxes of tin plates.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. There is no doubt they are your property? A. No, the boxes have our marks on them; he took the officer to Aggus' house to show us the man - he was bailed.
JAMES CRANSTONE. I am clerk to the prosecutors. On the day after he had been to our house, I went with Brown and Attfield to the prisoner's - we said, "We've come about some plates;" he said he had no objection to change plates with us, if we would give him others for them - he said he had bought them cheap, and they were too large; I took them to our place - Attfield asked him if the whole of the premises belonged to him; he said that was not a fair question - Attfield said, "I am an officer, and shall take you if you prevaricate so;" I asked if he had any receipt - he said No; a woman then came, and said she had an entry in a book, but it has no date to it; it states, "Bought nine boxes of tin plates, of Mr. Aggus" - I said if he would go and show us the person he bought them of, we would exchange them; we went to Mr. Aggus', but found nothing there - there was a cart in the place, with the name of J. Aggus, Pott-street, Bethnal-green, on it; a girl said it was her uncle's, and he was gone into the country to buy some pigs - the prisoner said the plates had been paid for in a public-house opposite, that he did not take a receipt, for he was rather tipsey at the time, but there was a person who could prove it - he named the person, but I forget the name. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-270]
1734. WILLIAM COLLIS & JOHN FARRELL were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of July, 2 pieces of woollen cloth, containing in length forty yards, value 40l., the goods of Thomas William Williamson.
JAMES GILL. I am warehouseman to Thomas William Williamson. On the 13th of July we gave Collis two cloths to deliver, and an order for two to be brought back; he did not return that day, which caused suspicion - they were to be brought from Mr. Rush's, Bridge-street, Westminster; he went out about ten o'clock in the morning - I think it was on Tuesday, and I saw him on the Saturday; his father called to say he had got his son, William, back - Collis had been in the prosecutor's employ about two years; I went, and saw him - he said he had obtained the cloths from Mr. Rush, and had by appointment met a boy, named Young; they took a coach, drove to Whitecross-street, and took them to the house of a man named Nathan, where they sold them for 10l. - they were worth upwards of 40l.; I have never seen them since.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Has Mr. Williamson any partner? A. No; no promise or threat was made him that I know of.
THOMAS MOORE. I am an apprentice to Mr. Rush. Collis brought him the order, and he gave him two pieces of cloth; it was on the 12th or 13th of July - I do not know what day of the week.
MAJOR BUSKIN. I am a hackney-coachman. I was on the stand opposite Adam-street, about two months ago- a youth came and engaged my coach to go to Whitecross-street; I went there with two youths and two pieces of cloth - I cannot swear either of the prisoners were the persons; I took them to the corner of a court - my coach is No. 1200.
JAMES SIVIANE. I am a butcher, and live at No. 192, Whitecross-street. On the 12th of July I saw the coach No. 1200 drawn up to the corner of a court; Collis took out a large roll of cloth, and a small one attached to it - I did not see any one with him; I followed him, and saw him take them to the second house on the left-hand, where fresh people had come; I went, listened, and heard a conversation in the room - I heard Nathan say, "What do you ask for the cloth?" I had known Nathan before - he had lived up the court; the reply was 40l.: I heard him say he would give 10l. for it - I came out, and met the coachman, who asked me where the lad had gone; I showed him, and then went to my own shop - I then saw Farrell join Collis; they stood opposite my shop, and looked at me - they then went together down Playhouse yard, and the coach went away; I afterwards saw the prisoners return in half an hour or three quarters of an hour, with each of them a bundle in their hands, and new hats on their heads - they went down the court, and came out again, with each a new suit of clothes on.
THOMAS VANN. I am an officer. I went with Waters to Shrewsbury-court, Whitecross-street, on the 25th of August; we found Farrell there.
THOMAS WATERS. On the 25th of August I went to the court, and found Farrell; I knocked at the door - his mother asked who was there; I told her, and she said, "I will make you wait till I dress myself;" I went up stairs, and asked if her son was at home - she said No, he slept there sometimes, but was not there then; I found a bed there which was warm; I looked, and found Farrell under his mother's bed, naked.
Two witnesses gave Collis a good character.
COLLIS - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Judgment Respited .
FARRELL - NOT GUILTY.

1735. ELIZABETH FREEMAN was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 7th of September, of a certain evil-disposed person, 4000lbs. of nails, value 78l.; 4500lbs. of tin plates, value 24l., and 17 shovels, value 2l., the goods of John Worrall Grazebrook and George Munro, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. CLARKSON (on behalf of the prosecution) declined offering any evidence.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-272]
1736. HENRY PECK was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of July, 4 sovereigns, the monies of Isaac Burrows; and HENRY TILSON was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
ISAAC BURROWS. I am a broker, and live in Upper Cleveland-street. On the 17th of July I hired a boat, from four o'clock in the morning till eight; I hired it at Westminster-bridge - Thomas Mellows and his son were with me; I saw a person laying drunk - I cannot say that it was Tilson - I hired the boat of Peck, and was to pay half a crown for it; he asked for security for it, and I gave him four sovereigns - he said his name was Cook; I said the boat had the name of Sawyer on it - he said it was his, and he had bought it of Sawyer a few days before, and that he would be there to meet us when we came back; I went out, and the boat began to leak - to save myself I took hold of one of the barges, and the boat went off; a waterman came and took me in - my two friends caught another barge, and they were brought back; when we came to the stairs I did not see Peck - I came again at eight o'clock, and staid till nine; he did not come: I gave the boat to the Thames Police, and mentioned the circumstance.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was Peck intoxicated? A. I cannot say - I am positive I was sober- there was a lad named Watts; I do not know whether he was sober - I do not know that I saw Tilson then; Peck was sober enough to stand, and to take my money - he did not deny having taken the sovereigns; I did not nearly upset the boat - we had had a pot of stout and two pots of ale, at Vauxhall, among four of us.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was Tilson the man laying asleep in the boat? A. No; I cannot say whether he was the man laying on the stairs - the man had his face down on his knees; I have not been trying to make any money of any friend of Tilson's - I have received 8l., but I hope I shall be allowed to explain it; since the prisoners were indicted a gentleman called in my neighbourhood, and made inquiries of my neighbours as to my circumstances, and left a card, inviting me to an inn in the City - his name was Waters; I went and saw him, and we got into conversation on this affair, how near I was being drowned - he said he supposed if he were to make me some recompence I should not be above receiving it; I said No, and he said, "Here are 8l.;" since then it has been said it was intended that I should not appear.
COURT. Q. What did you receive the 8l. for? A. I took it to be from some charitable fund - he did not tell me where it came from.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What were they to give it you for? A. It might be partly for my fright - I did not take it for what I said I had lost, and in lieu of my loss of time. Mr. Mellows was with me.
THOMAS MELLOWS. I was with the witness, and not in the boat; Peck is the man we had it of - he gave his name Cook; I was on the top of the stairs: my son came up, and said, "This man wants 4l. as security for the boat;" I said, "It is as much as the boat is worth;" I said to Peck, "What is your name?" he said Henry Cook - I said, "How comes the name of Sawyer to be on the boat?" he said, "It is all right - I bought it a few days ago;" when we got to Waterloo-bridge it began to fill with water - we got back; I waited from eight o'clock till twelve, but Peck did not come.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did Peck appear drunk or sober? A. He did not appear drunk - I was not drunk; upon my oath we did not run the boat against a barge, and capsize it - we had been to Vauxhall - as we came over the bridge we had a pot of stout, and some beef and ham in the garden; I cannot swear to Tilson's person - there was a young man named Watts on the top of the stairs; I could not tell whether he was drunk or sober - I did not hear Peck say he would rather accompany us than let us have the boat, unless we returned in an hour; I never received 1s.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were you with the prosecutor when he received the eight sovereigns? A. Yes - I do not know what possessed him to think that a charitable fund gave him the money; I understood it was for what he had lost, and the danger he had run.
JONATHAN SAWYER. I am a boat-builder. I let boats to watermen by the week - I saw this boat at the Thames Police-office; it was not an old boat - I examined it - it appeared to me to have been damaged by being launched; there was a hole which had been patched up with canvas and grease.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do not watermen lend their boats to one another? A. I believe they do allow others to take fares in them; the damage to the boat had been done in launching, not on the water.
WILLIAM WATTS. I am a waterman. I saw Peck and the gentleman talking, but I did not hear what they said; Peck came up to Tilson, who was lying on his face, and said, "Tilson, here is money;" but I did not hear what it was - Peck then said, "Row the boat to Waterlon-bridge;" I took them there - Peck then offered me a sovereign; I said I would have nothing to do with it - Tilson said, "Take it, it will do you good;" I said, "I will have nothing to do with it;" they took me to Covent-garden, gave me a cup of coffee, a slice of bread and butter, and a glass of gin; I left them at five o'clock, in Bow-street.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. So a sovereign was offered you? A. Yes; I was not sober - Peck was drunk, but not so much as I and Tilson were; I was on the top of the stairs at Westminster-bridge, lacing up my boots; I never was in trouble myself - my master was taken before the Magistrate about some coals, the sweepings of some barges; I went with him - my master had a month at Kingston; I was sent home - I never was in prison; I was taken on this charge by Mr. Little - he said, "Watts, you must step into our boat;" I was examined before the Magistrate - I told the truth.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was not Tilson exceedingly drunk? A. Yes; I could not say he knew what he was about.
ALEXANDER MITCHELL. I am a Thames Police-surveyor. I took Peck on the Sunday; I found but 6d. on him; he said he had received the money and spent it, and had but 6d. left - he said Tilson had part of it.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was he sober? A. He was half drunk then; I saw Mr. Burrow's about eleven o'clock in the day - he appeared as if he had been either flurried or drinking; I sent Mr. Little after Cook, but he could not find him - Mr. Mellows did not appear drunk.
JOHN LITTLE. I am a Thames Police-surveyor. I took up Tilson on Sunday, the 18th of July; I said he was wanted for letting out a boat - he said he was asleep on the stairs, that Peck came, awoke him, showed him some money, and gave him part of it, but he was so much in liquor he did not know how much; I asked what became of it - he said they cut his fob, and took 13s. out, which they took to his wife; his wife was present, and she said she had made away with all but two half-crowns.
Peck's Defence. I was very much in liquor, and do not know what I was about - Watt's took a sovereign, and returned it.
Tilson's Defence. I had been drinking.
Peck received a good character.
PECK - GUILTY. Aged 29.
Confined Fifteen Days.
TILSON - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-273]
1737. WILLIAM WESLEY and THOMAS BROWN were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of August, 1 purse, value 3d.; 26 yards of ribbon, value 6s.; 5 handkerchiefs, value 9s.; 3 pairs of gloves, value 2s.; 1 dozen of buttons, value 6d.; 1 purse, value 1s.; 2 sovereigns, 1 crown, 5 half-crowns, 1 shilling, 1 sixpence, and one 10l. Bank note, the property of John Jones.
JOHN JONES. I am a haberdasher, and live in Tottenham-court-road - Wesley lived with me, and Brown lived in Marylebone-street. On the evening of the 7th of August a Police-officer called, and produced three lengths of ribbon which had my mark on them - I knew them, but I cannot say exactly how lately I had seen them - I went to Mrs. Collier's, where Brown lived; we found Brown, and asked him who he had received them from; he said from Wesley, but did not say when or where - we then found on Brown five silk handkerchiefs, which he said he got from Wesley to take care of for him; we took Brown to the Police station; I went home - Wesley was out; we broke open his box, and found gloves and other things which are mine; when he returned we showed him the things, and asked when he stole them - he gave no answer; the officer searched him, and found 1s. 6d. in his waistcoat pocket - and fastened to his braces was a kid purse of mine, in which was the 10l. note, two sovereigns, and a crown-piece - I gave him into custody; the note is one I had received on the 29th of July, and marked it - it had been in a cash-box in the desk, but the key was left in it; we left him in the shop, making up grosses of buttons, when we went to dinner, and he said he went through the room, saw the key in the desk, and took it out.
Cross-examined by MR. STURGEON. Q. Did you mark the note yourself? A. Yes.
ELIZABETH MARY COLLIER. Brown was in my employ - Mr. Jones came to my shop about him; some property was found which had been brought to me by a person named Gosling - there were four lengths of black ribbon; I took charge of them for a few minutes, and then sent them out to three different linen-drapers - I asked Brown if they were his - he said Yes, that his mother sent them by mistake with his clothes; I believe these are the ribbons - I gave them to the Policeman, who gave them to Mr. Jones.
JOHN MASON. I am a Police-officer. I went to take Brown; I questioned him where he got these things - he said from Wesley, to take care of for him; I found these handkerchiefs and these ribbons, and took them to Mr. Jones; Brown refused to tell where Wesley lived, but I found it out - I waited at the prosecutor's till Wesley came home; he acknowledged that he stole these things - I found tied to his braces a leather purse, in which was this 10l. note, two sovereigns, one crown, five half-crowns,and some other silver, which he acknowledged to be Mr. Jones' also.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hold out any threat or promise to him? A. No.
Brown's Defence. I knew nothing about them - he gave them me to mind for him.
WESLEY - GUILTY. Aged 13.
BROWN - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-274]
1738. HENRY LACEY was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of July, 6 shillings, and the sum of 8d., in copper monies, the monies of George Abledon, his master.
GEORGE ABLEDON. I am a baker - the prisoner was in my employ. On the 12th of August he took 6s. 8d. of Mrs. Bailey, of Hackney; I sent him out with bread that day - she pays on Monday, and had two quartern loaves the week before; there was a little girl in my shop when he returned; I was just opposite - I asked him if he had paid; she said No - he went through into the bake-house: on the 8th of July, I sent him with some bread, which he did not deliver - he had delivered some to Mr. Bailey on the 7th; here are five quartern loaves down in the book on the 8th, but he did not deliver them - he asked for the five loaves, and I delivered them to him.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Did you deliver the five loaves to him? A. Yes, on the Thursday; she never has bread on a Thursday, but he said she wanted eight; there are no names in this book, but he goes to four customers regularly in order, and we know by that who has them; he has entered the five on Thursday - mistakes have occurred with him before; he has come home short of bread sometimes - I thought at first it was a mistake, but afterwards I found he had sold them at 6d. a piece; I am obliged to attend in my shop, as my wife is ill - this young woman comes over an hour or two in the afternoon; the prisoner's book was on the shelf on the Friday where he always puts it - I had looked there before, and it was not there.
COURT. Q. Did you ask on Friday for the money? A. No - I did not expect it till the following week.
SARAH BAILEY. I am a customer of the prosecutor's; I have bread on the Monday, Wednesday, and Friday - I ordered none on the Thursday; I had paid the prisoner on the Monday; he did no charge for these five loaves - he did not come on the following Monday.
DAVID DEARMAN. My mistress gave me the money to pay on the morning before, and I paid the prisoner.
THOMAS VANN. I was sent for to the prosecutor's on the 17th of July; he charged the prisoner with robbing him of fifteen loaves; he said he had left ten at Bailey's, and put the money on the counter - two half-crowns, a shilling, a sixpence, and two pence; he said he knew nothing of the other five, but I said, "Try to recollect who you paid it to;" he said "The young woman was coming from the passage to the shop, and that was the very money I received from Bailey."
Cross-examined. Q. He was in his master's place? A. Yes.
COURT to GEORGE ABLEDON. Q. Did you charge him with fifteen loaves? A. Yes; the ten Mrs. Bailey had the week before, and the five on the 8th - he said he had paid Martha Bright for the ten, but I did not ask about the five- I knew she had not had the money; I gave him in charge for having the five loaves, and not delivering them.
Cross-examined. Q. He was still in your service? A. Yes; on the Saturday I sent for the officer - I had not told him before the officer arrived; I keep no books - it is a ready money business; the money is laid down - there is always some person within call to take it.
Prisoner's Defence. My master states he is in the habit of paying attention to his business, but he is always in the Black Boy playing at cards - he is a pugifist; I was in the habit of taking five loaves on Monday, five on Wednesday, and five on Saturday to Mrs. Bailey, and if any other customer wanted them she had to go without till Saturday, as she knows.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-275]
1739. JOHN JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of July, 1 sovereign, the money of Richard Payne, from his person.
RICHARD PAYNE. On the 17th of July I was in Union-street, Hackney-road, at half-past eleven o'clock in the evening - I had been to a hard week's work, and took a little drop of drink; I had fallen asleep in the street- I had changed a sovereign just before.
WILLIAM SMITH. I saw the prisoner in the White Bear gardens, at half-past eleven o'clock at night; the prosecutor was lying on the ground, and the prisoner persuaded him to get up, but he would not - there was another row; I went, saw the prisoner go over to the prosecutor, help him up, and they fell down together - the prisoner then got up again, took the sovereign out of the prosecutor's pocket, and put it into his own; he asked me what it was - I said a sovereign; he said it was better in his pocket than in the prosecutor's - he said so help him God he should have it to-morrow; the prosecutor said,"It is all over, it is all over," and then he went away.
WILLIAM HARTGA. I was going by, and saw the prisoner take something from the prosecutor and put it into his left-hand pocket - the prosecutor said it was all over, and went away; the prisoner said so help him God he would give it him the next morning.
HENRY SOMES. I am a publican. The prisoner came to change a sovereign with me, and the officer came and took him - he said he had picked it up in the street.
GEORGE COLLYER. I took the prisoner at the Gentleman and Porter - he offered me the change; I said, "I want the sovereign;" the landlord said, "This is the sovereign" - I made him give the change back; he paid the 1 1/2d. for a glass of gin out of his own money.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming home, and the good man was laying down asleep, very much in liquor - the two boys were trying to lift him up; I tried to lift him, but could not - I took the sovereign, and said I would keep it till the morning; I told the boys where I had lived for thirty-five years.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 65. - Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18300916-276]
1740. HENRY MAYHEW was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of September, 1 clock, value 10s., the goods of George Dormer.
GEORGE DORMER. I am a wheelwright. On the 13thof September I missed this clock when I went home to dinner at one o'clock; it was there at eight when I went out - I have never seen it since; I knew the prisoner, he is a clock-maker, and lived in Somers'-town.
MARGARET DORMER. I am the prosecutor's daughter. On the 13th of September the prisoner came, and said my father had sent him for the clock - I refused to let him have it; he said my father was at the Black Cap, Camden-town, and he met him for it - I went to the Black Cap - my father was not there; I said he should not take it - he said he would, and in taking it down he tore the pendulum out of my hand and took it away - he gave no reason for it.
JOHN GLEESON. I took the prisoner. He at first denied all knowledge of it, and then acknowledged he had taken it to another person, who had not returned it.
Prisoner's Defence The prosecutor and I dined together - he gave me orders to go and take his clock, and repair it.
GEORGE DORMER. I never dined with him that day - I had seen him once before, and he repaired it.
GUILTY. Aged 46. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-277]
1741. JAMES SHAW was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of August, 9 handkerchiefs, value 1l., the goods of Lawrence Kennedy.
PHILEMON HARVEY. I am in the service of Mr. Lawrence Kennedy - I heard these handkerchiefs were stolen, and went to the door; I saw the prisoner running - I went and took him; these nine handkerchiefs were found in the church-yard, by the side of which he had ran - I had seen them in the morning, and this was between twelve and one o'clock.
JAMES SCOTCHMORE. On the 14th of August I saw the prisoner running down Cox's-lane, about four doors from the prosecutor's - I did not see him do any thing; I went down the lane, saw the handkerchiefs laying in the church-yard, and picked them up - I saw the prisoner stopped as he was running.
ROBERT STOREY. I saw the prisoner take off his hat, and heave these handkerchiefs on the ground, not far from the prosecutor's door - I took it up, and was going to give it him, but the prosecutor's young man came out, and I gave it him.
MARY MITCHELL. I was looking in the prosecutor's shop - I heard something crack, and the prisoner was taking the handkerchiefs from the door - he took them off the nail, and ran; he dropped a yellow silk handkerchief, about ten yards from the door - that was one I saw him take.
Prisoner's Defence. There were a great many people running - I ran, and a sailor stopped me.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-278]
1742. JOHN THOMPSON and EDWARD HUMPHREYS were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of July, 1 coral necklace, value 5s., the goods of Samuel Walter Burgess, from the person of Samuel Walter Johnson Burgess .
JOHANNA BURGESS. I am the wife of Samuel Walter Burgess. My son is two years and two months old, his name is Samuel Walter Johnson Burgess - I had a girl to take him out, on the 29th of July, about seven o'clock in the evening, to walk up and down in Canonbury-street; she came back and said the necklace was lost.
SARAH TUTTON. I took the little boy out; he had a necklace on his neck - two boy s took it off, but I did not see it taken; I saw the two prisoners running across the fields together after it was taken.
JOHN BRUNKER. I saw Thompson go up to the child, and take the necklace; Humphreys was a little further behind, throwing stones at me to keep me back, that I should not go and tell the girl - they then ran away; I went and told her - she went, took the child to her mistress, and then ran after the boys; the necklace has not been found - Humphreys was about half a dozen yards from Thompson, and he said, "One go one way, and the other the other."
JOHN HEBBITT. I saw the two prisoners; Thompson took the necklace off the child's neck - Humphreys was behind, heaving stones to keep us away; they went down to the bottom of the street, and ran aross the fields - Thompson put the necklace into his pocket.
JOHN SHEPHERD. I took up the prisoners the next day; they both denied it.
Thompson's Defence. I know nothing about this prisoner.
Humphreys' Defence. I was ill in bed with a sick head-ache.
WILLIAM BAKER. I am a labourer, and live in Sweetapple-court, Bishopsgate-street. On the 29th of July I came home from my work in the afternoon, about four o'clock; Humphreys was then ill in bed - his mother lodged there; I do not know that he got up afterwards - I was in the one pair, and he was in the bottom floor; I believe it was on a Thursday - I heard from his mother on the Friday night that he was taken up; I did not tell his mother I had seen him on the Thursday.
ELIZABETH HUMPHREYS. I am the mother of Humpheys, and I will be on my oath that he was very ill in bed on Thursday, the whole day; it was in July - I cannot say the day of the month; I went to work on the Friday, and when I came home, I heard he was taken - my husband is a porter at Mr. Bousfield's; Baker saw my son in bed, and he was remanded to bring witnesses, but I did not consider it would come to any thing - Baker had been out of work, and owes me 9s.; I told him nothing would come of it.
JOHN BRUNKER re-examined. I had seen Humphreys the day before, and almost every day.
JOHN HEBBITT. I am sure Humphreys was one of the boys - I had seen him before.
SARAH TUTTON. I am certain he is the boy; I had seen him for twenty minutes while I was walking with the child.
THOMPSON - GUILTY. Aged 16.
Whipped and Discharged.
HUMPHREYS - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-279]
1743. WILLIAM STEVENS was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of August, 1 saw, value 8s.; 2 chisels, value 1s.; 2 brad-awls, value 6d.; 4 gimblets, value 6d., and 3 screw-drivers, value 2s., the goods of John Field; 1 saw, value 4s., and 2 chisels, value 1s., the goods of Samuel Doyle .
JOHN FIELD. I am a carpenter. I lost my tools on the 4th of August, from Staples' Inn, where I was at work - they had been safe at twelve o'clock, and I missed them at half-past twelve.
SAMUEL DOYLE. I was working at the same place, and lost a saw and two chisels.
WILLIAM HENRY WALSHAM. Between twelve and one o'clock, on the 4th of August, I was passing Staples' Inn, and saw the prisoner coming across the garden, with a basket; I am a bricklayer, and seeing he was a stranger, I asked what business he had there; he said, a man at the end of the street had sent him for his tools - I asked him to go and show me the man - we went, and he could not find him - he then said, "Pray let me go - I have a wife and seven children;" I said I thought I should not be justified, and took him to the Police.
THOMAS PERRING. I am a Police-officer, and took the prisoner; he said a man in black gave him 6d. to go and fetch his tools.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I had been where there were some men at work, and a man asked me to go and open a gate, that I should find some tools in a basket under the trees, and he would give me 6d. to bring them down to him; I met this man, who told me to go and put them back, which I did.
Witness. He did not put them down.
GUILTY. Aged 35. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-280]
Third Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1744. JAMES TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of June, 1 ass, price 2l., the property of William Wilkins.
MARTHA WILKINS. I am the wife of William Wilkins, he lives at Bedford. I was going to Hornsey, and met the prisoner with an ass - we had lent it to Mr. Smith, at Harpenden, on the 17th of May - I knew it well - I had driven it hundreds of miles; Mr. Smith was to keep it a month, and in June he sent word he had lost it - the prisoner had it in a cart; I looked at it - he said to me,"You look very hard at it, do you want to buy it?" I said "No - but where did you buy it?" he said, "Of a man named Blake, at Barnet;" I said, "Buy it of who you would, it was stolen from Mr. Smith, and it is mine;" I went to a Policeman, and asked if he knew the man, he said No - but did not notice the name on the cart - I said No, I did not.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you not make any mistake about the ass? A. No, never - I did not describe one which did not turn out to be mine - I described it as a small, genteel black ass - and one was produced which had a white face, and mine was a black one - I have never seen mine since - the one produced was not like mine.
COURT. Q. How long after you lost it did you see it again? A. Not till July.
- DEATH. I live with Mr. Smith - I knew the ass; it was a black one; it was turned out in the grassfield on Saturday night.
WILLIAM HOLT. I am a Police-officer - the prosecutrix told me about this - I saw the prisoner in the junction-road, the next day, and took him - he said he had sold the ass, but he did not know to whom - she did not say it was hers, but she thought so. NOT GUILTY.
