[trialAccount t18300916-311]
1794. DANIEL KEEFE was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of September, I handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of Thomas Cheshire, from his person.
THOMAS CHESHIRE. I live with Mr. Graham, in Holborn. On the 13th of September, between six and seven o'clock in the evening, I was in Holborn, about twenty yards from king-street; a boy gave me information - I looked, and saw the prisoner and another boy running away; I pursued, and never lost sight of the prisoner - he was stopped about three parts of the way down King-street, by Yates; I was calling Stop thief! - he said he was not the boy, and denied having any handkerchief; I found it concealed under his coat - it was safe in my outside coat pocket when I left home ten minutes before.
Cross-examined by MR. FRENCH. Q. Was the other a tall boy? A. Rather taller than the prisoner; he ran behind the boy, but I noticed the prisoner most, as I noticed the position of his arm, and that he had the handkerchief under it - I felt my pocket the moment the boy told me; I did not feel it taken - it is not probable that it could have fallen out of my pocket; I did not feel it taken.
JOSEPH YATES. I am a tailor. I heard the prosecutor call stop thief! and saw the prisoner coming along alone - I stopped him; the prosecutor came up and said he had stolen a handkerchief; he said, "I am not the person; I have got nothing about me;" but the prosecutor took it from under his arm, between his coat and shirt.
Cross-examined. Q. He was alone? A. Yes, when I saw him; I caught him in my arms as he ran; he did not say he had picked it up, but that he had nothing about him.
CHARLES FIELD. I am a policeman. I heard a cry of Stop thief! at the corner of King-street; I turned round and saw Yates take hold of the prisoner - I was at the further end of the street.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I picked it up - I denied having it, not knowing he was the right owner.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 14. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-312]
First London Jury. before Mr. Recorder.
1795. GEORGE SMITH and JOHN LEWIS were indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
BENJAMIN JONES. I am a slopseller, and live at No. 10, Glasshouse-yard, Minories. I had known the prisoner Smith five or six days before this transaction; I had advanced him money - I am in the habit of advancing seamen who come into port, money or clothes before they receive their wages, and selling my goods at a profit as others do - on the 19th of July the two prisoners came to my house; I was not at home when they came, but when I came in Smith said, "Jones, I promised you if I should fall in with any of my shipmates, I would bring them to you to let them have clothes till pay-day" - he said Lewis was Thomas Morgan; I am sure of that, and sure that Lewis is the man - he introduced him as wanting clothing till pay-day, which I gave him - I knew Smith belonged to the Lady Melville, an East Indiaman, according to his own account, and I wrote to Mr. King to know if any body named Thomas Morgan was a seaman on board that ship; I was satisfied that there was - Lewis put his mark, as Morgan, to the order for clothes, in the presence of Smith; I have it here - in consequence of Smith's representation, I believed Lewis to be morgan, and furnished him with a jacket, waistcoat and trousers, two pairs of shoes, a cotton shirt, a black silk handkerchief, a bat, and a cotton handkerchief; Smith said to Lewis, "Tom, you owe me a pair of shoes on the voyage, and while you are here you may as well let Jones give me a pair, and put them down to you" - I am sure he called him Tom; he had them, and I put them to Morgan's account - I had a few days before advanced Smith 30s., which he repaid me afterwards at the India-house; I have never been paid for these clothes.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. From your manner of detailing the transaction, I take it for granted the prisoners were the only persons present? A. William Smith, the poulterer, of the same ship, was present - the prisoner, Smith, introduced him also as a customer- Henry Solomons and John Emanuel were also in theroom transacting my business; Smith paid me the 30s. about the 22nd or 23rd; I am not a crimp, and do not know what is - I should like to have it explained; I always understood it to be a recruiting serjeant for the army - a person who sends his servants on board ships to sell goods before the sailors have their wages, is not a crimp - I know Daniel Price; he was not present, I swear - not in the apartment I was transacting business; he was down below - he went before the Justice of his own voluntary - if it had not been for him, I should not have known that Morgan was Lewis; the Lord Mayor said there was no occasion to take his recognizance - he was examined and sworn; I cannot say whether what he said was taken down; Emanuel is a person who comes to transact business with me, and he at times does business for me; he at times brings a man to fit him out, and he received a benefit from it - he is not in my employ; he did not go on board the Melville on my account - I never sent him; I know he was at Gravesend, but to what ship he went I cannot say - he was not there on my business; he did not advance money to the prisoner for me - he is not in partnership with me, nor my agent; Smith borrowed the 30s. of me about the 10th or 12th of July, but I have not my book, and cannot tell the date - it is entered in my book; it was paid three or four days after the 19th; when the prisoners came I was out at Leaf, Son and Cos; Old Change, purchasing goods: Solomons and Hart keep my books; William Smith had 1l. in money, and a suit of clothes, on the 19th of July, and paid on pay-day.
Q. You was not at dinner when this happened? A. No; a pot of porter was sent for while the order was made out, for Morgan to send to King, the agent of the Melville, to ascertain if money was due to Morgan, and on receiving the answer I gave him credit; I have the note here - no more porter was drank, to my knowledge; I sent for no more: I do not know Pesman and Quicksey- I have heard of them, and went to school with them; I know where one of them lives, but was never at either of their houses; William Smith's debt was between 5l. and 6l. - Lewis signed the order on the first floor, in the room where he received the clothing; it was in an open room, not in a corner - Smith stood over him, and saw him do it; Solomons wrote the name of Morgan, and Lewis put his mark to it; William Smith and the prisoner Smith were both in the room during the transaction, but whether William Smith went down afterwards I cannot say.
Q. Do you remember William Smith calling to either of the defendants after going down? A. Not going down, but in the room; he told Lewis not to take any clothing, as he did not want any - he did not call him by any name; he did not call them when he was down stairs - he did not call out, "Smith, are you coming," to my recollection.
Q. And was not the answer "Yes, directly I can get Lewis?" A. Never; the name Lewis was not mentioned, or I should not have transacted the business: nor did Smith afterwards say, "Lewis, are you ready;" no such words passed - I have made loans to perhaps twenty or twenty-five of the Melville crew; I cannot say exactly - I am sure there were not fifty; I had no dinner that day - I never mind dinner if I can get money - they all three went up stairs together, to be supplied with clothes; Lewis was sober, he put his clothing on when I gave it him - he had the same jacket, shirt, and trousers on when before the Lord Mayor.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Lewis did not appear as he does now, almost naked? A. He had a frock on; Solomons saw him sign the paper as Morgan - the advances made to you came to 5l. exactly.
Lewis. Q. Was I ever in your house before this occurrence? A. Not to my knowledge, but I have so many seamen; I never sent an agent on board the Melville, who advanced you 10s.; I did not authorise King to advance 10s. to him on my account.
HENRY SOLOMONS. I was at Mr. Jones' when the prisoners were there; I wrote the order, and read it out to them - this is it (looking at it) - the name of Thomas Morgan was written first, and Lewis then put his mark to it; Jones appeared sober, and so did both the priprisoners - the order was read out to Lewis; I then asked him to put his name to it - he said he could not write, but could make his mark; I then asked his name - he said Thomas Morgan; I accordingly wrote Thomas Morgan; he then made his mark to it - I am certain he did that in Smith's presence.
Cross-examined. Q. Who was present? A. Mr. Jones, the two prisoners, Emanuel, myself, and Smith, the poulterer; I am not acquainted with Emanuel's business - I believe he has business with master at times - I know nothing of his being master's agent for the Melville; I saw him assisting in taking out the goods the men were to have - I cannot say whether he had any profit from them; I never saw Lewis before the 19th - I never knew Emanuel advance money on master's account; I never heard so - I saw Daniel Price there on the 19th, before the prisoners were served with the clothes; he was not in the room, but down stairs - he was before the Lord Mayor; I cannot say who took him there - he gave some evidence; I do not know what crimp means - I understand it is a recruiting serjeant; I do not under the term bumboating.
Lewis. Q. Previous to that day, did you not accompany me from Jones' to King's, with a note to check 10s. off my account which was paid me by Jones on board the Melville? A. No, I never did; I never saw any Thomas Morgan at Jones' but yourself - Price was at the house on the 19th, but I cannot say whether it was at the same time as you, if any thing, it was a few minutes before - I was there when you first came in; I do not remember Jones being at dinner - I think he was at home when they first came, but I cannot speak with certainly.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. In what part of the house were you when you were called down to the prisoners - were you in the same room with them when they came? A. Yes; I do not recollect whether Jones was there; I was not at all intoxicated.
CHARLES MORRIS KING. I am agent for the Lady Melville; I knew Thomas Morgan, a seaman on board; neither of the prisoners are that man; both the prisoners performed a voyagein that ship, and came to England - they knew Morgan, he performed the voyage with them; I never knew Lewis by the name of Morgan; the ship arrived on the 10th of July, and I paid Lewis that very day 16l. 1s., which were the wages due to him in full - an order was sent to me respecting the wages due to Thomas Morgan - there was above 5l. due to him then; he was a quarter-master - Lewis is a common seaman; I know he can write, for I produce a power of attorney which he signed in my presence - I am the subscribing witness to it; he signed " John Lewis;" this was on the 10th of July - there is no resemblance between Lewis and Morgan; Morgan is an infirm old man.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known Smith? A. Six or seven years; I know nothing against him; I knew him in the Princess Charlotte and another vessel - I know Emanuel; I cannot tell whether he was agent to Jones - neither he nor Jones received any part of Lewis' wages.
Lewis. Q. Was not Emanuel on board the vessel before it came up to Gravesend, with Jones' direction on pieces of paper? A. Emanuel was sent on board the ship by me, and advanced Lewis 10s. to pay his expences ashore - he came to me subsequently, and received the balance; Jones had nothing to do with it - Emanuel was there as my servant.
Q. Was I put to bed drunk in your house? A. No; you were perfectly sober, and the moment you had the money you left, and said you were going to take the Bristol coach to go to Ireland - I met him a few days after; he said he had lost his wages, and I gave him half a crown.
Lewis. I was put to bed drunk at his house, and can prove it; I was robbed of every farthing of my wages in the house - King and Jones are partners - Jones' notes mention his name and address; he said his practice was, that part of what he advanced should be spent in his house. Witness. I keep a public-house at No.55, St. Mary-axe; he never complained of losing money there; he left directly he was paid - when I met him afterwards, he did not say he was robbed in my house.
JURY. Q. Which of Jones' young men brought you the order to obtain the money belonging to Morgan? A. That I cannot tell; this slip of paper was produced to me to ascertain what it was due to Morgan, not for me to pay it; I turned to the ledger, and sent an account of the balance.
HENRY SOLOMONS. I wrote this inquiry note about the wages - I did not take it to King (read)
Sir, - Please to deliver to bearer my note for balance of wages due to me for services on board the Honourable Company's ship Lady Melville. T. MORGAN X his mark.
MR. KING. Morgan can write - his receipt for wages is in Court; he received his wages at the India-house on the 22nd - I was not aware till that time that he could write, and it frequently happens that seamen are disposed to write, and at other times to make a mark - I have no doubt Jones' cards were on board the Melville; I do not know whether Emanuel distributed them - there are cards of a great many slopsellers distributed as soon as a ship arrives.
RICHARD COOPER. I am witnessing clerk in the pay-office, at the East India-house. on the 22nd of July 18l. 9s. was paid in my presence to Thomas Morgan, of the Melville - he signed the receipt in my presence - he was quarter-master: there was no other Thomas Morgan on board - Lewis is not that man.
Lewis. Q. Who received my wages? A. John Wright, by power of attorney - Lewis had had them before.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. The company pay on a particular day? A. Yes; the agents may advance it before.
MR. KING. I paid Lewis 16l. 1s. on the 10th of July, by virtue of the power of attorney - the persons advancing it would receive it on the 22nd, at the India-house.
MR. CLARKSON addressed the Court and Jury on behalf of the prisoner Smith.
Lewis' Defence. Before I came ashore King and Jones' agents were on board, and each man thought proper to accept of 10s. on condition that they were to leave their things in their custody till pay-day; I was one of the unfortunate number - I gave my clothes, hammock, and bag, to Jone's man, and never saw a stitch of them again - I went up to Jones' the very day I went about this, and spoke about it - there were eight Jews in the house; they knocked me down, tore my things off, and locked me in the room - I went out for an officer, and then they ran out; I spoke about my clothes, and they gave me in charge; when pay-day came I never received a farthing, except 3l. which I received from King - I got drunk in his house that morning, and had but 5s.; I have not received a farthing more, though I have been away nineteen months, not even the clothes I brought ashore.
WILLIAM SMITH. I was on board the Lady Melville; the prisoners were in the same vessel - I did not see a man come on board at Gravesend; I went, on the 19th, with the defendants to Jones'; Lewis was drunk - Jones was at home up in his room when we got there; Smith told Jones he had brought him two men that wanted money and clothes - a crimp is a man who comes on board on purpose to take poor sailors in; Jones said he was very much obliged to him - some porter was sent for; it was about three pots - I am sure there was more than one; we all partook of it, Jones and all - Jones said he could not wait on us just then, for he was going down to take his dinner; he took us down with him into his room, and then he sent for the porter - I did not see Emanuel there; I saw Daniel Price and Solomons; we were all in the room with Jones while he dined, and then I and Jones went up; we did not all go up together - I had a jacket, trousers, waistcoat, hat, and a sovereign, which came to between 5l. and 6l.; I paid him when my wages became due; and as I came down stairs I met Smith, Lewis, and Price going up into the room; I had left Jones and Solomons in the room - I went up in about half an hour and called out,"Jack Smith are you ready;" he told me he was coming as soon as Lewis was ready - that was loud enough for Jones and Solomons to hear; I went down, then went up again, called Smith, and he said he was coming as soon as Lewis was ready - it was loud enough for any body to hear; I heard Lewis tell Smith he owed him a pair of shoes, to take a pair, and he would pay for them - I was on the top of the stairs; Emanuel is now outside the Court- I did not see him in the house; I went before the Magistrate one day - Price was there.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You are Smith, the poulterer? A. Yes - I know Thomas Morgan; he was gunner's mate, not quarter-master - he is an old man: there was only one Thomas Morgan on board - Lewis was quite drunk whenhe went to Jones; Jones told him several times to hold his tongue, for he did not know what he was talking about.
Q. Was he so drunk that an honest man would not like to make a bargain with him? A. No, I should not - he staggered about the street when we first came out; Jones investigated who I was before he gave me money - he did not inquire if any money was due to me; Lewis recollected about owing Smith the shoes, drunk as he was.
COURT. Q. Did you tell him he was too drunk to make a bargain? A. No, nor did Smith say so - he was sober.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you not, on the pay-day at the India-house, say to Jones, "I hope you don't think I had a hand in the business?" A. I did not; Jones said,"Smith, I don't want you, I think you are an honest man."
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did Smith, in your hearing, call"Lewis Morgan,"? A. I did not hear him; I was present when he introduced him - he did not use any name.
Lewis. Q. Did not both you and Smith call me by my own name while Jones was at dinner? A. Both of us did.
SMITH - GUILTY. Aged 30.
Confined Six Months.
LEWIS - GUILTY. Aged 38.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-313]
NEW COURT. THURSDAY, SEPTEMBER 23.
Third Middlesex Jury. - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1796. JOHN SMITH and WILLIAM HALL were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of September, I handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of David Lewis, from his person.
DAVID LEWIS. I am a medical man. On the 16th of September, as I was in St. John's-square, Clerkenwell, I felt something pull at my pocket; I turned and saw Smith putting my handkerchief into Hall's bosom - I had had it half a minute before; I seized Hall, and Smith ran away - I called Stop thief! he was brought back in about a minute; I had lost sight of him, but I am sure he is the person.
Hall. Q. Can you swear I was with Smith? A. Yes; the handkerchief was put into your bosom - I seized you, and took it from you - you wanted me to let you go, and said you knew nothing about it.
CHARLES DENNIS. I was coming through Jerusalem-passage, and saw the two prisoners walking just a-head of me; Mr. Lewis was walking with two others - when he got to a broad part, Smith took the handkerchief, and put it into Hall's bosom; Lewis seized Hall - Smith ran off; I pursued, cried Stop thief! and he was stopped without getting out of my sight - I had seen them together for one hundred yards.
Hall. Q. Can you say I was with Smith? A. Yes, and when I came up to you, you said, "You did not see me take the handkerchief."
WILLIAM HILL. I stopped Smith, who was coming towards me.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Hall's Defence. it was thrown at me, it lodged between my coat and waistcoat; I know nothing of Smith.
SMITH - GUILTY. Aged 15.
HALL - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-314]
1797. WILLIAM WINDRED was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of August, 1 watch, value 1l.; 2 seals, value 4s.; 1 chain, value 2d., and 1 key, value 1d., the goods of John Snell; and SARAH MYERS, was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MARIA SNELL. I am the wife of John Snell. On the 26th of August I lost a watch from a drawer, in the room behind my shop, at the corner of Spital-street, Spitalfields - I had seen it safe twenty minutes after nine o'clock, and missed it a quarter before ten, when I went to the drawer to look for a bill; I had been out for about five minutes, to carry half-a-bushel of coals to the next door, and in that time Windred came in, and left a bag which I had sent with coals to his mother the day before - I did not see him in my shop.
ELEANOR TAYLOR. I was cleaning the step of our door, which is next to the prosecutor's - I saw Windred in Snell's shop, he said to me, "Ellen, tell Mrs. Snell I have brought the coal-bag home;" he went into the shop, and might be there five minutes, but I went in doors, and did not see him come out.
WILLIAM NELSON. I work with my mother. On the 26th of August, I saw Windred opposite the prosecutor's door, at the corner of the street, at half-past ten o'clock - he said he had a watch, and asked me if I knew where to sell it - I said No - I went with him down Petticoat-lane, where we met a man, who told us where Myers lived; we went to her house and saw her - he asked her if she would buy it - she said Yes; he asked her 1l. for it, and she gave him 8s., - she gave us each 4s.; we left the watch and came away - when he came out he saw his mother, and ran away; at night the officer came for me, but I was not at home - Mr. Brown came to me, and I took him to Myers.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you mean to say that Mrs. Myers ever gave one farthing for the watch? A. Yes - she did not ask him where he got it, nor did he say his father gave it to him - I did not take it to Myers; I am sure we were together - she did not stop the watch for him to bring his father; she bought it as soon as we took it - I carried the money about me, and chucked it away; there was no other woman in the room; but there was when I went back with my mother to the house; I would not have done any thing in this business for the world, if I had thought it was wrong - I forget how often I have been taken up; I was kept to hard labour in the House of Correction - it is about six months ago; I was there three months - I am now twelve years old; I was sent there because I went to a chandler's shop to buy some liquorice, and the man swore I went to steal his till - I was never committed for stealing a watch; Mr. Cobley accused me of stealing one; there were two other boys with me - we were not taken up.
COURT. Q. Did Mr. Cobley get his watch? A. Yes, and then he said we did not steal it.
Windred. He took the watch himself, put it into his sidepocket, sold it himself, and gave me 4s. Witness. I could not take it - two boys came and called me down from my work; I then met him, and he told me of it.
Windred. Mrs. Snell's girl had the watch in her hand, playing with it, putting it to her ear; he watched where sheput it down - they went and took it. Witness. I did not.
JOHN EAGLE. I took Windred opposite Whitechapel church - he denied all knowledge of this.
JAMES BROWN. On the 30th of August I went and saw Mrs. Snell, and got the watch; I went to Myers', she was sitting in Harrow-alley; I told her what I wanted, and asked if she knew any thing of the watch or the boy - she said he left it at her house; but she had at first denied it; when she saw Windred she said he left it on the window, but she gave him no money - I found 1s. 9 1/2d. on Windred.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not she deny having bought it? A. She denied all knowledge of it at first; she had given up the watch two days before - I understand she gave it to Windred's mother.
MRS. SNELL. It was brought by Mrs. Windred and Mrs. Nelson; my little girl is six years of age - there was a large table in front of the drawers, which I am convinced she could not move.
Myers put in a written Defence stating, that Windred had brought her the watch, saying that his father sent him to sell it, and she had detained it till his father should come himself, she had given him no money, and that he was alone.
MRS. JACOBS. My husband deals in glass. On the 26th of August I was in Myers' shop - I saw Nelson pass; he turned round and said to her."Will you buy a watch?" she looked at it and said," How came you by it?" he said, "My Father is in trouble, and desired me to sell it;" she said," I will detain the watch till your father or mother comes;" it was a silver watch.
MYERS - NOT GUILTY.
WINDRED - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-315]
1733. SUSAN GRAY was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of July, 1 purse, value 1d., and 2 half-sovereigns, the monies of William Rumball, from his person.
WILLIAM RUMBALL. On the 10th of July I met the Prisoner at Enfield-highway; I walked four or five poles with her - I saw no one near me but her; I did not go out of the road - I looked at my purse, saw the two half-sovereigns, and put it into my pocket; when she was gone I felt and missed it - I followed, and caught her in the road- I asked for my money; she said she had not got it - I held her, and sent for a constable: I have never seen my money since - it was about twelve o'clock at night.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Were you quite sober? A. Yes - I did stop with her for about ten minutes; I had put my money into my right-hand pocket - the constable took her on the Monday morning following; I sent the man who came up for a constable, and up came five or six men, who released her from me, and knocked me down - she got away.
COURT. Q. Did you go up a lane with her? A. No; I stopped, but did not loosen any part of my dress - I had been drinking with my partners, where we were paid for our work.
JOHN WILSON. I took the prisoner on Monday morning; I found nothing on her - she said she had not had it, nor seen the man; when I got up on the Saturday night there were four or five men round the prosecutor - he was laying down, and when he got up he was all over blood.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you find the prisoner? A. At her mother's; I went there on Saturday night, but could not find her - I found her in bed on Monday morning.
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw any thing of the kind - he knocked me down on the road, and would pull my right-hand pocket out; there were 3s. in it - nobody touched him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-316]
1799. WILLIAM TAYLOR was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the shop of William Fowler, on the 10th of September, and stealing therein 2 sets of harness, value 9l.; 3 bridles, value 14s.; 1 chain bridle, not quite complete, value 14s.; 1 pair of traces, value 1l.; 1 back-band, value 5s.; 7 halters, value 17s.; 1 tug and trace, value 18s.; 1 pair of shaft-tugs, value 10s.; 1 cart back-band, value 5s. 6d.; 1 pair of winkers, value 14s.; 1 chain trace, value 7s.; 9 bridle-bits, value 2l. 10s.; 13 brushes, value 30s., and 4 curry-combs, value 5s., the goods of the said William Fowler .
HENRY MASKELL. I am apprentice to Mr. William Fowler, a saddler - his shop is in the New-road. on the evening of the 9th of September I left it safe, and went to High-street, Hampstead, where my master lives - about six or seven o'clock next morning a Policeman came, and I went to the shop; I found the sets of harness and other articles in two sacks - I had left them hanging on pegs; they are all my master's.
JOHN LAUDON. I am a watchman. On Friday night, the 10th of September, between twelve and one o'clock. I was going round my beat, from Tottenham-court-road to the New-road - I tried the doors and windows, and found the door of this shop open; this padlock was hanging to the door- it had been picked; I pushed it open, and saw the prisoner standing inside the shop; I asked him what brought him there - he said he was Mr. Fowler, from Hampstead, and he was going to shut up his shop - I said he was not Mr. Fowler, for I knew him; I met the policeman, and gave him into custody - another Policeman then came with a light, and we found these two sacks of harness; the prisoner said he was going to Hampstead, and if I would go with him he could prove he was Mr. Fowler; at the station he gave his name as William Taylor, St. John-street-road, Clerkenwell.
Cross-examined by Mr. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you find on him any one thing that could pick a lock? A. No - I did not ask him if he was Mr. Fowler; he said if I would go to Hampstead I should find it was all right - I told the Magistrate that, and I suppose it was taken down- he was sober.
JOHN RODERICK. I am a Policeman. I was left in charge of the property while the prisoner was taken to the station-house; another officer came, and I gave the property into his possession.
WILLIAM NUTTALL. I relieved Roderick.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been to the Camden Arms, Kensington; I then went to the Yorkshire Stingo, and waited there; I then got on the Paddington coach - it came on wet, and I got down; I went to the King's Head - I saw two gentlemen, who are here, and staid drinking with them there, and then at the Adam and Eve; one ofthem said, "I have a friend who keeps the Southampton Arms;" we went there, and had some more to drink - I was very much in liquor, and left them; as to the shop, I know no more how I came there than your Lordship.
THOMAS BUTCHER. I keep an oyster-shop, in Holborn. I know Tinson, a tailor, who lives at No. 131, High Holborn - he is the son of the landlord of the Old Bailey public-house. On the 9th of September I saw the prisoner at the King's Head; we staid there about an hour, and then went to the Adam and Eve - he left my company about a quarter-past eleven o'clock; he was drunk, and no one who saw him could avoid seeing that he was so - Tinson was with us; we had a great deal of brandy and water - it was a wet night; we were all nearly drunk - I went to get a coach, and when I came back the prisoner was gone; I have kept my present shop five years, and lived seven years eight doors farther on - I have known the prisoner fourteen or fifteen years; he is a hackney-master, and has had a very good character.
THOMAS TINSON. My father is landlord of the Old Bailey public-house. I was with the witness and the prisoner at two or three public-house; we parted from the prisoner a little past eleven o'clock - the house was about shutting up; the prisoner was drunk.
COURT. Q. Were you drunk? A. I cannot say I was sober - I was not drunk to lose my recollection.
JOHN LAUDON re-examined. Q. Did the prisoner appear to you drunk? A. No, and if the inspector had been bound over he could prove the same.
JOHN RODERICK. He did not appear to me to be drunk.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-317]
1800. JOHN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of July, 2 pistols, value 2l., the goods of John Thomas; and that he had been previously convicted of felony.
JOHN THOMAS. I am an auctioneer. On the 29th of July I lost a pair of pistols from Eccleston-street, Pimlico - I had not seen them for some time; they were deposited with me, with other property, for sale by auction - I took possession of the house and property three or four days before the sale; I know they were there, from the inventory and catalogue - I do not know the prisoner.
FREDERICK JACKSON. I am a porter, and live in Phoenix-street, Soho. I have seen the prisoner at different sales; the furniture was for sale on that day - I tied a pair of pistols to each of two blunderbusses; I did not particularly notice them - I had occasion to go down from the second floor, and I asked my fellow-servant whether any body was in the house; he said No; I went up again, and missed one pistol from each blunderbuss - I went out, and took the prisoner a quarter of a mile from the house, with something wrapped up in a blue handkerchief under his arm; I said, "I want the pistols;" I opened the parcel, and they were in it - I gave him to the Policeman; he begged I would let him go - I said I would not, as I had been robbed so many times.
WILLIAM AGGS. I received the prisoner and the pistols.
THOMAS TAYLOR. I was employed to lot the goods; I know these are one of the pairs of pistols - there was another pair and two blunderbusses.
HENRY GODDARD. I have a certificate of the prisoner's conviction on the 15th of April last; I know he is the person - (read).
GUILTY. Aged 37. - Transported for Life.

1801. JOHN RYAN was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of August, 1 watch, value 2l., the goods of Dennis Neal.
DENNIS NEAL. On the 3rd of August I left my watch in my sister's room, No. 12, Gee's-court, between seven and eight o'clock in the morning - I returned between seven and eight in the evening; I then missed it - I had seen the prisoner at the door when I went out in the morning - I saw the watch afterwards at the office; I am certain this is it - it was my brother's.
Prisoner. Q. Had not I a right to be at the door? A. I cannot say; I had been but two days there - I saw you and another boy.
JAMES MOODY. I am a pawnbroker. This watch was pawned with me by the prisoner, on the 3rd of August, between one and two o'clock.
Prisoner's Defence. I was standing at the door, and a young man came and said, "Will you earn 1s.?" I said Yes; he said, "Pawn this watch;" I said, "Why do you not do it yourself?" he said he did not like to go in.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.

1802. ANN HERWICK was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of September, 1 shirt, value 4s., the goods of John Brickell.
WILLIAM HENRY NORRIS. I am a pawnbroker, in the service of Mr. John Brickell. On the 1st of September the prisoner took a shirt from his shop, and walked away with it - it was very near the door; I went out after her; she hesitated, and said she would not have it - I told her to keep it in her own hands - I sent a young man who had been watching her some time for an officer.
Prisoner. I was not away from the spot where the shirt was - it hung round in a court. Witness. She had gone some yards before I took her.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that she had taken hold of the shirt to examine it, and it fell down, but she had not moved from the shop.
GUILTY. Aged 24. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-320]
1803. JOHN HEMMINGS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of July, 1 purse, value 6d.; 2 sovereigns, 2 half-sovereigns, 4 half-crowns, and 3 shillings, the property of Robert Chisholme, from his person.
ROBERT CHISHOLME. I lost my purse on the 9th of of July - I do not know exactly where, but I believe it was in the crowd going into the Haymarket-theatre - I felt it in the crowd, and took 6s. from it without taking it out of my pocket; my friend paid for me - I heard the constable cry out."Has any body lost a purse?" I found I had lost mine - I knew I had one sovereign and two half-sovereigns in it.
Cross-examined. by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What are you? A. A traveller; I reside in the United States - I have been here about three months; I did not say at first that I should not know the purse, nor that I would swear to it by two steel rings which were rusty; I recollect several days before having had two half-sovereign in mypurse, and that morning I took a sovereign and put in with them; I did not say at first there were two sovereigns and some silver - I said one sovereign and two half-sovereigns; on the purse being opened, there were two sovereigns, two half-sovereigns, and 13s. in it; I cannot swear that the other sovereign had been there or that it had not - I heard the constable call, "Has any body lost a purse?" I am positive I did not say that I did not know that it was the one I lost.
JOHN SHAW. I was going down the Haymarket, and saw a great many people going in: I heard some one say,"Take care of your pockets;" I looked round, saw the prosecutor and several others, and all in a moment I saw the prisoner thrust his hand into the prosecutor's pocket, draw something out, and put it into his left-hand breeches pocket - I followed him up the gallery stairs, and seized him; Groom took the purse from him - I went back, and saw the prosecutor; I thought I knew him again, but not being able to get to him, I called, and on the second time the gentleman said, "I have, it is a new purse, and two rings a little rusty;" I asked him what money was in it- he said he was not certain, he believed towards 4l.; he was certain two half-sovereigns were in it.
Cross-examined. Q. What did you say? A. If any body had lost any thing, if they would apply at the pitdoor they would hear of it; the prisoner was going up stairs when I saw him - the prosecutor said he had been laying out some money; he said at the office there was one sovereign and two half-sovereigns, which he had put in just before he came out - he did not mention three sovereigns; I am sure he said the rings were rusty - Groom took the purse from the prisoner's pocket, and has had it ever since; I scarcely suffered half a minute to elapse before I took him.
JOHN GROOM. I was standing against the theatre about six o'clock in the evening; there were a great many of what they call the swell mob - Shaw turned and said the prisoner had got a purse; I followed him, and Shaw held him while I took this purse out of his left-hand trousers pocket - I took him to the watch-house, and Shaw brought the prosecutor; this purse has the same money in it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did Shaw say any thing to you? A. Yes, and I saw something blue in the prisoner's hand- Shaw said it was in his breeches pocket; he ran up the gallery stairs - the prisoner said it was his own; I asked him how much was in it, and he said between 3l. and 4l.; he had taken some loose silver out - he was taken almost instantly; he could not have taken it out to see what was in it - there was 3l. and some silver in it; he had 9s. in his pocket besides; he told me almost exactly what was in it - I believe the prosecutor said there was a sovereign, two half-sovereigns in it, and some silver, but he afterwards recollected there was another sovereign he had taken out in the morning; that was after it had been opened and shown to him - he said he had brought a third sovereign out to pay a bill, but had not paid it.
ROBERT CHISHOLME. This is my purse; I had bought it a day or two before - I know the rings; the money was in it as it is now.
Cross-examined. Q. Upon your oath, was it not after it was found that you said you brought out a sovereign to pay a debt and had not? A. I said I partly recollected bringing out a third sovereign, but I did not recollect it at first - I bought it in Catherine-street; I gave half a crown for the purse and rings; I put these old rings on the purse, as those I bought were too small - I said they were old; I did not write down every word I said.
Prisoner's Defence. I had a blue purse, two sovereigns, two half-sovereigns, and some silver in it, to what amount I cannot tell; there was a great crowd - I stopped about ten minutes, and ten or twelve persons came behind me; when the doors were opened, I was forced against the side of the theatre - I took some silver from my purse, and intended to pay to go into the pit, but being such a crowd, I changed my mind, and thought I would go into the 2s. gallery; I was stopped - the purse was taken from my pocket; Shaw said he could not say he saw me rob any one - I said I would go, but it was my purse; I went to a public-house with Groom, and then to the watchhouse, till Shaw and Groom came to fetch me; I said,"Where am I to go?" they said to the Magistrate, as there was a gentleman had lost his purse; but Shaw said, "I am not going to swear that I saw you take it, or that it is his property;" the purse and money are mine.
JOHN SHAW. I did not say so; he asked me if I was to prosecute, and I said I did not know - he said, could I make it all right, as it was no use to Police him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you tell the Magistrate that? A. Yes.
JOHN GROOM. I did take him to a public house; he said he was very dry, and I let him have some beer.
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-321]
1804. HENRY HUGHES was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of August, 1 half crown, the money of John Ion.
SARAH ION. I am the daughter of John Ion. on the 31st of August I was going along Old-street; the prisoner sells fruit there, and I gave him half a crown in mistake for a half-penny, for some plums - I dropped it in his basket; I found it out in two minutes, and went back- he said he had not got it, and had not half a crown about him; he said the officer might search him if he chose, which he did, but could not find it in his pockets - he then took off his hat, and found it there.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Who gave you the half-crown? A. My sister; I did not look at the money as I gave it him - I had 7s. 9 1/2d. in my hand; the prisoner had plums in his basket - I did not see it drop in; the plums very likely covered it - I did not state that I gave it him - I threw it; I have said I did not think the prisoner had any idea of stealing it - the officer made me prosecute here to-day; the Magistrate sent a warrant - I was unwilling to prosecute - the officer did not scold me; he said it was his duty to take him when he found the half-crown upon him after his denying it.
WILLIAM ROBERTS. I am the officer. This girl applied to me - I went up to the prisoner; she was entreating him to return her the half-crown which she had given in place of a half-penny - I said, "If you have the half-crown, return it back;" he said he had no half-crown in his possession - he then turned out his pockets, and said,"If you doubt my word, search me;" I searched hispockets, but found nothing - I took off his hat, and the half-crown fell down; I said, "I must take you into custody;" he begged hard, and said if I would let him go he would do so no more - I said I must do my duty to the public.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not know the half-crown was there; I had sold all my fruit but 1d. worth.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1s. and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18300916-322]
1805. JAMES HANNIGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of August, 1 ring, value 3s., the goods of Amelia Darcy; and 1 brooch, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Frances Gibson.
AMELIA DARCY. I am single. I was out of a situation. The prisoner used to come to see his aunt, Mrs. Hannigan, where I was lodging; on the 29th of August I missed the ring, which I had seen safe the Tuesday before, when I took it off my finger and gave it to Mrs. Gibson to take care of for me.(Property produced and sworn to.)
FRANCES GIBSON. I am the wife of Richard Gibson. The witness gave me the ring, and I put it into a box with a brooch of my husband's; I saw them both safe on Saturday, and the prisoner's brother asked me if they were gold- they were both missing on the Sunday; this is the brooch - it is my husband's.
SAMUEL HASLOCK. These two articles were pawned with me, on the 28th of August, but to the best of my knowledge not by the prisoner: I gave a duplicate for them, in the name of Francis Smith, King-street.
THOMAS BRADLEY POLLINGTON. I am a Police-serjeant. I took the prisoner on the 10th of September; I said it was for a brooch and ring - he said he knew nothing about them, but in the lobby at the office, he said he could tell me all about it; I cautioned him not to say any thing to me - he then said he should not have done it but for his brother, and he had pawned them with the witness; I went and found them there.
Prisoner. I am guilty, but it was through my brother - I beg for mercy.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Four Months.

1807. JOSEPH DAVIS and JAMES LOMAS were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of September, 1 handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of a person unknown, from his person.
JOHN STANILAND. I am a tailor. On the 12th of September I saw the two prisoners together in Oxford-street, between eleven and twelve o'clock - I saw Lomas take a handkerchief from a gentleman's pocket; Davis was by his side - I crossed to the gentleman, and asked if he had lost any thing; he said Yes, a handkerchief - this was in Dean-street; we went into Oxford-street, and followed the prisoners till they were taken into custody - I do not know who the gentleman was; the handkerchief was found in Davis' hat, but Lomas took it.
Davis. Q. Did not the gentleman say he could not swear to it? A. No, he said he would not have the trouble.
JOHN TAYLOR. I crossed, and took the prisoners to the station - I found the handkerchief in Davis' hat; the gentleman said it was his.
Davis. Q. Did not the gentleman say he could not swear to it? A. He said he should not swear to it, nor have any more to do with it.
Davis' Defence. It is my own; I was going to the coach rank to see for my brother - I met this young man; I know no more of him than a stranger - I have seen him once or twice among the hackney-coaches; he said he would go with me, and we went to get some water - the Policeman came and took us; he took off my hat, and found the handkerchief - the gentleman said it was impossible to swear to it; I had it clean in my hat - my sister washed it for me.
DAVIS - GUILTY. Aged 20.
LOMAS - GUILTY. Aged 20.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-325]
1872. MATILDA BRITTON was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of July, 1 shawl, value 8s., the goods of Sarah Coward, from her person.
SARAH COWARD. I am single. I was in Walworth on the 21st of July, and the prisoner took my shawl off my arm, between one and two o'clock - I knew her before, but she was out of my sight immediately.
BENJAMIN BIRDSEYE REEVE. I am a pawnbroker. - On the 21st of August this shawl and collar were pawned with me, by a woman, in the name of Martha Foggat - I cannot swear to the prisoner.
THOMAS ASHTON. On the 4th of September I was called by the prosecutrix to take the prisoner, as she was going to Camberwell fair - I said to the prisoner, "How came you to steal the shawl?" she made no reply - I said,"What have you done with it?" she said she had pawned it in Tottenham-court-road, at the second shop on the left hand.
Prisoner. She lent me the shawl to put on my neck.
SARAH COWARD. No, she asked me, and I refused - she then snatched it off my arm, and ran away; I heard no more of her for a fortnight.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in her company an hour; and if it was not for such infamous characters as her, there would not be so many poor wretches here - she keeps a bad house in the Almonry, Westminster, and deludes young girls in; I lived there three weeks.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-326]
1809 JOHN BLACKMORE, JOHN WILSON, and WILLIAM SELBY were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of July, 8 seals, value 2s., the goods of Edward Wallis.
JANE THOMPSON. I am assistant to Mr. Edward Wallis; he is a jeweller, and lives in High-street, Islington. On the evening of the 21st of July, at a quarter-past seven o'clock, Blackmore came in for change for a shilling; I said I had not change - he left the shop instantly, and as he left he swept the counter with his arm, and took a card of eight seals; I went round the counter, and called thePoliceman, who took him into custody - I did not see either of the other prisoners.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Might not this he a mistake? A. No, they could not have been taken off by mistake - I was serving two ladies at the time.
JAMES PILGRIM. I keep an eating-house in Rufford's-buildings. On the 21st of July the three prisoners came into my shop, at a quarter-past seven o'clock, and ordered three basins of soup; my daughter served one - the Policeman came in, and ordered one after another out of the box; they dropped these seals under the table.
JOHN SHEEHY. I am a Policeman. I was going by the shop, and met Perry, who gave me information; he said they had gone into Pilgrim's shop - I went and saw the three prisoners at the table - Wilson asked what was the charge; I told them to keep their hands out of their pockets; I heard something drop on the floor, and found these seals - it was almost opposite the prosecutor's.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Did you order them out? A. Yes, out of the box they were sitting in; I do not know who dropped them - the box was not very large.
WILLIAM PERRY. I was near the prosecutor's, and saw the prisoners - Selby gave Blackmore a shilling, and he went into Jones', the tallow-chandler's - he then came out, and went into the prosecutor's; he came out with a card like this, and showed it to them - they then ran after him down to the Peacock; they then returned, and went to the eating-house; I told this lady, and she told me to tell the Policeman.
Wilson's Defence. I was not there at the time - I had just left my work, and met these two prisoners; I told Selby I would take my supper at the cook-shop - he said he would go with me.
Selby's Defence. I had left my work, and met Blackmore; I owed him 4d., and gave him 1s. to get change - I then met Wilson, who said he was going to an eating-house - we went, and were taken.
BLACKMORE - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Whipped and Discharged.
WILSON - GUILTY. Aged 17.
SELBY - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.

1810. WILLIAM STUBBS was indicted for feloniously receiving, of a certain evil disposed person, on the 13th of September, 15 yards of fustian, value 13s., the goods of James Rogers, well knowing it to have been stolen.
JAMES PARTRIDGE. I am in the employ of Mr. James Rogers, a linen-draper, at Brentford. On the 13th of September he lost a piece of fustian, in the evening; I did not see it taken - the witness told me of it; I ran out, and saw the prisoner walking up the street - I went close up to him; he seemed to have something under his smockfrock; I went back to the witness, and said, "Is that the man?" he said Yes; I cried, Stop thief! he ran, and I pursued him - the Police-officer took him, and the fustian was picked up in the road; I did not see him drop it.
PATRICK SHEEHAN. I was passing the shop, and saw the prisoner and another man; the other took the fustian, and gave it to the prisoner - I went, and told of it at the shop; the prisoner hid it under his frock - he was pursued, and taken.
WILLIAM PEASGOOD. On Monday evening, the 13th of September, I was on duty, and heard Stop thief! called I turned, and saw the prisoner running - he ran into a corner, and I took him; there was a stage-coach going by at the time - he crossed the road; this fustian was picked up.
JAMES PARTRIDGE. This is the property.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you refuse to swear it was the property? A. I did at first till I was certain of it - I do not remember saying I could not swear to it; I might at first.
Prisoner. I was running after the man that was running - there was time enough to have got away if I had chosen; Sheehan was told he should be paid if I was convicted.
PATRICK SHEEHAN. No, I said I could not stop from my business, and the Policeman said, "You will be paid your hire;" he did not say any particular sum.
JURY. Q. What are you? A. A labourer; I wanted to return to my own country - the Magistrate said I should be paid my expences; I had only come to this country in July.
JOHN BOARDS. I am a pawnbroker. I came here on another trial - I have known Sheehan by sight for three or four years; he has come to my shop - I think not this year.
JURY. Q. Are you sure you are not mistaken? A. Yes - I thought directly I knew him; he has pawned things at our place.
COURT to JAMES PARTRIDGE. Q. Was it possible for Sheehan to have taken this, and thrown it down where it was picked up? A. I do not know where it was picked up - I saw nothing but a stick in his hand.
Prisoner to WILLIAM PEASGOOD. Q. Was it not possible for me to get away? A. If he had known the place he might - our serjeant did say I had done a good thing for myself; I have supported Sheehan ever since - he was bound over to appear, and there was no one else to support him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-328]
1811. JOHN PRIZEMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of July, 10 pieces of timber, value 20l., the goods of John Chatfield and others.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM TEMPEST MERCER. I am in the employ of Messrs. John Chatfield and others, who live in Upper Ground-street, Blackfriar's-road. On the 5th of July we missed ten sticks of timber, which I had seen safe at Battersea on the 1st of July - on the 6th I went to Smallwood's, and found five whole pieces, and one piece partly cut; there was the ship's name, the number of the float, and the contents of the piece on them.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. What was the mark on them? A. The ship's name was the Latona; we did not have the entire cargo, but several pieces; the rest would have the same ship's name on it, but the number would be different - there might be some others of the same contents; I did not see it from the 1st to the 5th of July - the dock theywere in is secured by a boom, which goes across; the timber generally floats at high water - it can be removed without loosing the boom.
Re-examined. Q. Did you find the locks of it broken? A. One was a little damaged - the same feet of timber may be in one stick as another, but the number would not agree.
WILLIAM SUMMERSELL. I am a waterman, and live at Hammersmith - the prisoner is a waterman. On the 3rd of July, about twelve o'clock at noon, I saw him with ten pieces of timber, which he was hauling into a dock at Lane's-end; I afterwards saw Smallwood and the prisoner at the Cannon public-house; the prisoner put on a canvas-frock - I saw the same timber there on the Sunday, but I did not see it taken away; I afterwards saw Prudent speak to the prisoner the same day, and then Prudent rowed the boat down to Hungerford.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you always state the same time? A. At Bow-street I said three o'clock, but it was twelve- I was not aware of any thing coming of it; I judge now by the state of the tide - I was in the Old Court as a prisoner; I had done the same as others, nothing, but I went to New South Wales, and staid there seven years - I was charged at Horsemonger-lane, but not in the Court; I was a prisoner at Croydon, but the bill was thrown out.
WILLIAM PRUDENT. I am a waterman, and live at Hammersmith. I saw the prisoner there as I was going to London, and I saw Summersell; it was on a Saturday in July - I was an apprentice at the time, and I asked the prisoner to give me a cast down in his boat; he said he was going to stop a little while, but I might row his boat down, leave it in charge of somebody, and he would call for it in the morning - I rowed myself down, and left it alongside the others.
WILLIAM EDWARDS. I am a carman to George Cook, a coal-merchant. I received charge of some timber on the Saturday, from Smallwood, for whom I was at work - there were one or two persons present; I will not say whether the prisoner was, or no - I took the timber from Mr. Smallwood's premises.
COURT. Q. Was the prisoner present, or not? A. I believe he was, but I never saw him but that once.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Upon your oath, did you not say he was? A. I believe he was; I received ten sticks of timber - I took eight to Black Lion-lane, and two to Mr. Cook's, my employer; I kept those two for expences till they were taken away - I began to deliver them at eight o'clock in the morning, and might finish about twelve or one; the timber I carted on Monday was that I received charge of on Saturday.
SAMUEL TAUNTON. I took Smallwood into custody - I did not take the prisoner; I went to Smallwood's premises on the 7th of July; I found five logs and one piece - they were behind some houses; I saw Edwards there.
MR. MERCER. I accompanied the officer to Smallwood's; I did not see Edwards there - I found five sticks there; I then went to Cook's, where I had been the preceding evening, but Edwards was not at home - I found the two sticks there through the people on Smallwood's premises; the two I found at Cook's, and the five at Smallwood's, are part of the ten that were lost.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-329]
1812. WILLIAM HENDEN was indicted for embezzlement.
SAMUEL WHITEWELL. I am a green-grocer. I occasionally employ the prisoner; he received money, and was to bring it me.
CHARLOTTE FLOYD. I gave the prisoner a sovereign for his master in June last, in the morning, at my house in Gower-street.
SAMUEL WHITEWELL. He never returned to me; I met him accidentally that day month - he said he had spent it. GUILTY. Aged 14.

1813. MARGARET McHENRY was indicted for embezzlement.
JOHN WATKINS. I am a milkman. The prisoner was in my employ to carry milk, and milk cows; she had been four years and five months in my service; she was to bring me the money she received sometimes.
Cross-examined by MR. FRENCH. Q. What do you mean by sometimes? A. She sometimes did not bring it all home; I did not sell a milk-walk to her, nor to any one altogether - we had had some talk about it, and the price was settled; she had began to sell milk on her own account - she got some of my customers, but I did not much mind that; she got but two or three houses - she has never said she had collected this money for me.
SOPHIA HENSON. I was a customer of the prosecutor's; I paid the prisoner 6d. and 1d., every week, regularly; the last payment was on the 1st of June.
JOHN WATKINS. She made no payment to me at all; she might have paid it at the end of a few days, certainly; she brought money from day to day, to be sure - I cannot tell respecting the days.
GEORGE AVIS. I took the prisoner on a warrant, on the 7th of September; she said she stopped it because they sent her home on Sunday night without money, and her husband borrowed some.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-331]
1814. JOHN ROBINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of August, 1 hat, value 2s., the goods of William Smith, from his person.
WILLIAM SMITH. On the 16th of August I was between Vauxhall-bridge, and Millbank; the prisoner knocked my hat off my head, and ran away with it - I saw him take it, but I lost sight of him: I went up, and the officer had my hat in his hand, in a minute or less.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were you sober? A. I had been with two friends, three of us had drank two bottles of port wine - I was not tipsy; I had taken nothing else; I am sixty one years of age - I had this wine at the Blossom's Inn; I had dined two hours before.
JOHN TUBB. I am an officer. I heard the prosecutor cry Watch! the prisoner ran from him - I stopped him with this hat on his head; I wished him to return with me, but he refused - he took the hat from his head, and said, "This is the hat I have taken from the gentleman; it is my first offence, pray have mercy upon me."
Cross-examined. Q. Did you tell the Magistrate that he said it was his first offence? A. He asked for mercy;I will not swear I did not say that he owned it was his first offence, but to the best of my knowledge I did not - it had slipped my memory; the prisoner had no other hat- the gentleman was not sober.
WILLIAM SMITH. This is my hat
Cross-examined. Q. Were you sober? A. Pretty well; I was sober enough to know I had been robbed.
Prisoner's Defence. I met the prosecutor with a young man, who asked me to go with him; I got on to Milbank, and he was so very obstinate I was forced to leave him - I went on and found the hat; I took off my cap, and put it on; I met the officer, who said he must detain me - the gentleman then came up without his hat, and said he had no knowledge of me, and I had not robbed him.
JOHN TUBB. Immediately I brought up the prisoner, the prosecutor said, "That is the fellow (or) the villain who took my hat."
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18300916-332]
1815. THOMAS SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of September, 1 sovereign, and one 5l. Bank note, the property of Robert Mitchell, from his person.
ROBERT MITCHELL. I am a sailor. On the 15th of September I was lying on a bed in a house in Albion-street, Commercial-road - I had been paid at the India-house, and was rather intoxicated; the prisoner was with me at the India-house - he was not in the room when I lost my property, but was under the same roof; the sovereign was in my right-hand trousers pocket, and the note in the other; I have not seen the note since - I have seen a sovereign, but could not swear to it; there was a girl lying behind me, but not asleep - she awoke me, and told me of it.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What time was this? A. I was a little intoxicated; I think it must have been between seven o'clock and ten - I was not so drunk I could not stand up; the woman was not drunk; I called an officer, and gave her into custody with two men - there was a person named Hardy there; I think I had been in three public-houses after I received my money - I will not swear I had not been in six; I had but one girl with me; there were three other girls in company; I had been twelve or thirteen days living at the prisoner's house, and owed him money, which I intended to pay when I got up; I owed him a bill of 6l. 12s. - it is still due to him; no one was in the room with me but the girl - I found the prisoner in the passage, and Hardy with him; I gave the girl in charge, and then then the two men as fast as I could speak - after the girl was in custody she said the prisoner had it; I do not remember seeing any money fall - I heard a woman say to the girl, "If you have got the money why don't you give it to him?" the girl was crying, and said she had not - she did not say she bad money, but not mine - this was in the room; I remember her going on her knees, and protesting she had not the money.
HARRIET CLEVER. I was living with the prosecutor at the prisoner's house - he came from the India-house, and said he had a 5l. note and a sovereign; he went on the bed to lay down with me between four and five o'clock; the prisoner asked him to pay him - he said he would as soon as he got sober; the prisoner then left the room, and came in a little afterwards, while the prosecutor was asleep, and felt in his left pocket, took a pocket-book out, and a paper out of it - he then felt in his right-hand pocket, and took out a sovereign.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-333]
1816. DENNIS SULLIVAN and JOHN KIRBY were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of September, 2 iron gratings, value 3l., and 1 iron frame, value 2l., the goods of Christopher Richardson the elder, and others, the Trustees and Commissioners of a certain Turnpike-road, there called the Commercial-road .
2nd COUNT, stating the goods to be the property of Christopher Richardson.
3rd COUNT, stating the goods to be the conveniences belonging to the said road.
FIVE OTHER COUNTS, varying the charge.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
JAMES BARNES. I am contractor for excavating the Commercial-road. I saw some grating and frames lying near Green Arbour-square, at six o'clock in the morning, on the 10th of September, that belonged to Mr. Richardson and the other Commissioner s - Sullivan worked for Mr. Rutter.
JOHN COLLINS. I am a wheelwright, and live in Little Turner-street, in the Commercial-road. Sullivan came to my house last Friday week, about two or three o'clock, and said he wanted a truck to move some sailor's things from the Halfway-house to Church-lane; I let it him - I found it again at the Flying Horse-yard near the office.
MATTHEW SKIPP. I deal in iron, and live in Church-lane, Commercial-road. Last Friday week I saw the two prisoners between four and five o'clock - they brought two iron gratings and one iron curb in a truck; Sullivan offered it for sale - I asked where they got it; he said he came from Mr. Robertson, the builder, at the back of the hospital - I said, "Why does not Mr. Robertson send a note that I might know who it came from?" Sullivan said I had no occasion to be afraid - I said I would know whose it was before I bought it; Kirby then said, "To tell you the truth, we picked it up in the Commercial-road to sell for some beer;" I said they might put it into the scale, and while they did that I saw the officer, and gave charge of them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you ever bought things of this kind before? A. No.
THOMAS SHELSWELL. I am an officer. On the 10th of September, I was near Skipp's shop; he took me in - there were these two gratings and frame - I asked the prisoners if they belonged to them; Sullivan said, No; I asked Skipp's men who brought them, and one of them said the two prisoners - I then asked Sullivan where he got the truck; he said, "From Collins, a wheel wright;" I put the prisoners into two different lock-up rooms, then went to Sullivan and said, "I suspect they belong to the Commercial-road Trust;" he said, "You have just guessed it?" I then went to Kirby and asked him where they got it - he said he knew nothing about them - that he was employed by Sullivan.
Cross-examined. Q. He said, "You have guessed it," not"You have guessed right?" A. No; and Kirby said he had helped the other - I asked them about it to get the truth.
Re-examined. Q. Did you make them any promise or threat? A. No.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GEORGE PARKER BIDDER. I am superintendent of the new works on the Commercial-road; Christopher Richardson, the elder, and a great many others are Commissioners; I know these to be ours, by the exact similarity; they are from a pattern made specially for the use of our road; Sullivan was employed on the road, but had left about a fortnight; he had been employed several months in pitching the road; he knew the road.
Cross-examined. Q. Is there any private-mark on the property of the Commercial-road? A. Not on the grating- Messrs. Hunter and English, of Bow, are the manufacturers; it would be a breach of trust in the manufacturers to send the same sort of grating to other people; the pattern is not common.
Re-examined. Q. Do you know of any road near the Commercial-road that has such articles on it? A. No; there is no other road undergoing any improvement.
Sullivan's Defence. A man came and asked if we wanted a job - we said Yes, and we went down to where this grating laid.
THOMAS FOWLER. I am a sailor. I was going down the Commercial-road, and about the Halfway-house I met these prisoners; I knew Sullivan, and stopped talking with him; a man came and said he would give him a job; he said he had a heavy load, and he must get a truck and another man; Kirby said, "I am here;" they went and got a truck, and he took them to where the grating was, and told them to take it to Back-church-lane.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Where do you live? A. In Essex-street; this was between three and four o'clock, on Friday, the 10th of September - I was in a public-house four days afterwards, and one of their friends came and asked the favour of me to come and say what I knew.
JAMES BOWMAN. I am a letter-founder. I was with the last witness, and we met the prisoners; I know Sullivan was one - I am not certain of the other; a gentleman came up, dressed in black clothes - he asked if they wanted a job - Sullivan said, "Yes, if you will employ us;" he said, "If you will get a truck, I will give you a job to carry some grating down to Church-lane" - Sullivan said he could get a truck and he must pay for it; he said he should want another man, and the other said, "Here I am."
SULLIVAN - GUILTY. Aged 28.
KIRBY - GUILTY. Aged 35.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18300916-334]
1817. JOSEPH MORRIA was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of September, 1 till, value 6d., and 5 shillings, the property of Sophia Miller.
SOPHIA MILLER. I keep an oil-shop in Spa-fields. Last Friday evening, between nine and ten o'clock, I was in the back parlour, and heard a noise of money; I went into the shop, and saw the prisoner going out with the till in his hand - it had 5s. and some halfpence in it; I am quite sure he is the boy.
WILLIAM HAYNES. I am a shoemaker. I heard the cry of Stop thief! I saw the prosecutrix picking up the till - she said the boy had run down the walk; I ran and caught the prisoner.
WILLIAM MARCH. I took the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 15. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300916-335]
1818. ELLEN EDINGTON was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of August, 2 handkerchiefs, value 2s.; 1 crown-piece, and 2 shillings, the property of John Foster, from his person.
ELIZABETH SALMON. I knew John Foster - he is gone to sea; I met him with the prisoner on the 10th of August, between ten and eleven o'clock; my sister was with me - he recognized me, and went to give us something to drink; he gave the prisoner a crown-piece to pay for the liquor - she put the change in her own pocket; we came out all together, and Foster said, "I have lost my money" - the prisoner then met another woman, and asked her to go into another public-house; while they were there the man said he had lost his money - I followed the prisoner, and saw her pay some money with some halfpence; I said, "You have robbed that man" - she made no reply; I went and told the prosecutor - I then went back; the prisoner was gone - I went and saw her; I said, "You have robbed that man" - she said it was of no consequence; I saw the Policeman, and gave her in charge - we went back to the prosecutor; he was gone to sleep.
Prisoner. Q. How many times have you been here? A. Never in my life.
CHARLES FIELDER. I took up the prisoner; I found 2s. 6d. in silver on her, and in her shoe a crown-piece - I found two handkerchiefs, which the prosecutor identified; he claimed them in her presence, and she owned before the Magistrate that she was guilty - she said nothing about the money.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not own to me that you heard this woman demand the money of me? A. No, I did not; on taking her to the station I said, "What money have you got belonging to Foster?" she said she had 2s.
Prisoner's Defence. I met the prosecutor - he was very much in liquor; he desired me to go down Gravel-lane - he bought some fruit, and put it into this handkerchief; we then went to a public-house, and had five pints of gin - the witness and another woman were there; he desired me to go and get some oysters - this woman came and demanded part of the 5s. piece, and she said if I did not give it her she would say I had robbed him.
JAMES LEA. I am an officer. Salmon has been tried here three times, and twice this year.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-336]
1819. SARAH DRISCOLL was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of September, 1 coat, value 2l., and 1 purse, value 2d., the goods of William Flyn.
WILLIAM FLYN. I am a labourer. On Tuesday, the 15th of September, I was in bed in Charlotte-court, Whitechapel, in my own room - this coat was in my box, and there was a purse there; they were gone in the morning - the prisoner came to see a lodger of mine, and slept there on the Saturday and Sunday night.
WILLIAM CATTELL. I am a pawnbroker. This coat was pawned with me by a woman, but I cannot swear who it was.
JAMES LEA. I am an officer. Yesterday week the prosecutor brought the prisoner to the office - he stated he had picked up this pocket, which the prisoner dropped, in theroad to the office: I found in it the duplicate of this coat, and another duplicate in the name of Flyn - the prisoner said the pocket did not belong to her.
WILLIAM FLYN. I took the prisoner, and as I was going along the street I saw this pocket drop, which had this purse and duplicate in it, and this other duplicate, which I had had in my box.
WILLIAM CATTELL. I gave this duplicate for the coat.
Prisoner. There was another woman, who claimed the pocket as her own.
WILLIAM FLYN. Yes, she was with me, and said she had lost it, with 14s. 6d. in it, from under her head.
ELIZABETH ACKROY. I saw the prisoner coming out of the house on the Tuesday evening, about nine o'clock.
Prisoner. Q. Where did you see me in the house? A. I heard some person creep up stairs, and then I saw you go out - it might be about nine o'clock.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, declaring that the pocket had not fallen from her.
GUILTY. Aged 29. - Confined Six Months.

1822. EDWARD CORCORAN was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of September, 3 sovereigns, 16 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of William Lewis Thornborrow.
WILLIAM LEWIS THORNBORROW. I have a small independence, and went to receive it on Wednesday, the 16th of September - I met the prisoner, whom I knew; I asked how he fared - he said but badly; I proposed to give him a good dinner and a shilling, which I did, and left four sovereigns in possession of the landlady; on the Thursday morning he called on me again, and asked if he could do me any little service - I said Yes, he might brush my clothes and clean my shoes; he took the papers out of my pocket, and found the note of the four sovereigns, which I had left with Mrs. Crouch - he took it, and got the balance; I had drawn 3s. 6d. the night before.
ANN CROUCH. The last witness left me four sovereigns and a friend of mine wrote this paper; I gave it to the prosecutor - the prisoner brought it next morning, and said he came for the money, which I gave him; there were three sovereigns and 16s. 6d. - he had had 3s. 6d. of me the night before.
Prisoner. He got drunk the day before, and I took him from this lady's house - he would have a cab, and sent me back for 3s. 6d. to pay for it; he then sent me back for 1l., but I could not get that - he told me to call on him the next morning, which I did; he got out of bed, and told me to sit down - he sent for some gin, and we had four half-pints one after the other; he had promised me some time before, that when he got this money he would assist me - I asked him then to do it; he gave me this paper, and told me to go and get it, and to pay him so much a week.
W. L. THORNBORROW. There is not a word of truth in it - I had not seen him for nine months before; I wrote a petition for him to get re-established in the Police, where he had been before, but lost his situation; he showed me some names of characters - he had an unfavourable answer sent back; I was perfectly sober.
ANN CROUCH re-examined. Q. Did the prisoner come back for 3s. 6d. the same night? A. Yes; he returned in ten minutes, said the man must have a cab, and requested me to let him have 3s. 6d., which I did - he wanted a sovereign, but I would not let him have that.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300916-341]
1823. SUSANNAH BURGETT was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of August, 1 silver rule, value 5s., and 1 ring, value 2s. 6d., the goods of John Woods.
MARY WOODS. I am the wife of John Woods, and live in Pleasant-row, Camden-town. The prisoner lived with me for eleven months and a fortnight - she left on the 20th of July; I missed several articles, and among the rest a ring.
ELIZABETH HIDER. The prisoner used to come to my mother's to wash - after she left the prosecutor she gave me a ring, which I think was this; I do not believe it is gold - my husband said it was not worth 1s. 6d.; I gave it to Mrs. Wood - the prisoner asked if it would fit me; I tried it on, and it did.
Prisoner. I said I found it, and it was not mine; if it was inquired for she was to give it up.
WILLIAM JAMES. I took the prisoner - I went to her lodging, at time o'clock in the evening; she was not at home - I waited till about ten; she then came, and said,"I understand you say I have robbed you;" the prosecutrix said, "Yes, and where is the rule?" she said, "I took it out of the fire-place" - she then asked where the ring was; she said, "I saw no ring" - we went to her lodging; she made use of very bad language, and said if the apprentice had given up the silver rule he was a d-d fool; she said she had wanted to go over the water for four years, and if the prosecutor would send her there it would save her the expence.
HENRY ATHERFOLD. I am the prosecutor's apprentice. The prisoner gave me this rule, and said she had it given to her - she gave it me for a keepsake; I lost it threemonths afterwards - I asked her for it; she said it was in the jewellery box - I said, "I will ask for it;" she said,"No, I will get it."
Prisoner's Defence. The child had it to play with, and it fell out of the box; I did not see it afterwards.
NOT GUILTY.
